=== NOTES 4:0 | The Universe and the Solar System – Origin Theories, the Sun, Planets, Asteroids, Comets, Meteors
## SUB 0 | Planets – Superlatives, Moons and Planet Facts
[0:0:0] # Superlatives (asked most)
[0:0:1] TABLE head: Feature | Planet
  [0:0:1.0] Nearest to the Sun, smallest, shortest year (88 days), fastest orbit, least mass | Mercury
  [0:0:1.1] Closest to Earth on average (stays nearest longest) | Mercury (APPSC 2019)
  [0:0:1.2] Hottest (~464°C, CO₂ greenhouse), brightest planet, Morning/Evening Star, Earth's twin, nearest at closest approach (41.4 million km) | Venus
  [0:0:1.3] Densest (5.5 g/cm³) | Earth
  [0:0:1.4] Red Planet; day 24 h 37 min and axial tilt 25°12' almost identical to Earth's (23 h 56 min; 23°30') | Mars
  [0:0:1.5] Largest, heaviest (~318 Earth masses), fastest spin (~10 h) | Jupiter
  [0:0:1.6] Least dense (0.7 g/cm³, less than water); most famous rings; looks yellowish; second largest | Saturn
  [0:0:1.7] Rotates on its side; lowest single temperature (~–224°C); blue-green (methane) | Uranus
  [0:0:1.8] Farthest planet, coldest on average (~–200°C), longest year among planets (164.9 yr) | Neptune
  [0:0:1.9] Retrograde (east-to-west) rotation | Venus and Uranus
  [0:0:1.10] No natural satellite | Mercury and Venus (APPSC 2019: Venus)
[0:0:2] - **Earth's twin:** **Venus** – close to the Earth in **size, mass and density** (APPSC G1; asked 4 times).
[0:0:3] - **Hottest = Venus, not Mercury** (thick CO₂ atmosphere traps heat although Mercury is nearer the Sun); **Saturn is warmer than Neptune** (nearer the Sun).
[0:0:4] - **Nearest planet to Earth:** traditional key **Venus** (closest approach 41.4 million km), then **Mars** (78.4 million km) when Venus is not offered; on a time-averaged basis Mercury is closest.
[0:0:5] - **Brightness:** Venus is the brightest planet, but the **Moon** is brighter than Venus, so "Venus is the brightest object after the Sun" is **false** (CDS 2009 A-R: A false, R true).
[0:0:6] - **Rings:** **Saturn** (famous), but **Jupiter, Uranus and Neptune also have rings** – "Saturn is the only ringed planet" is false.
[0:0:7] - **Atmospheres:** Jupiter, Saturn = mostly **hydrogen and helium**; Uranus, Neptune = H, He + **methane** (blue-green tint; "ice giants"); **Mars = carbon dioxide** (95%), so "H, He and methane on Mars" is wrong.
[0:0:8] - **Mars water:** a **subsurface lake** of liquid water under the **south polar ice cap** was reported in *Science* on **25–26 July 2018** (MARSIS radar, Mars Express).
[0:0:9] - **Pluto** (dwarf planet since 2006) has the longest year of all (**247.9 years**); it was farther in than **Neptune** from **1979 to 1999**, when Neptune was the farthest.
[0:0:10] # Moons
[0:0:11] TABLE head: Moon | Planet | Fact
  [0:0:11.0] Ganymede | Jupiter | Largest moon of the solar system (5,268 km), bigger than Mercury
  [0:0:11.1] Titan | Saturn | Saturn's largest (Rhea 2nd); thick nitrogen atmosphere; methane lakes ~100 m deep (APPSC 2019)
  [0:0:11.2] Europa | Jupiter | Ocean under an icy crust
  [0:0:11.3] Io, Europa, Ganymede, Callisto | Jupiter | Galilean moons (Galileo, 1610)
  [0:0:11.4] Phobos, Deimos | Mars | Mars's two moons
  [0:0:11.5] Charon | Pluto | Largest of Pluto's 5 (Charon, Styx, Nix, Kerberos, Hydra)
  [0:0:11.6] The Moon | Earth | Earth's only natural satellite
[0:0:12] - **Most moons: Saturn** – 274 after 128 were confirmed (March 2025); **March 2026**: the Minor Planet Center added 15 (**11 Saturn, 4 Jupiter**) → **Saturn 285, Jupiter 101** (as of 2026).
[0:0:13] - **History of the "most moons" key:** **Jupiter led until 2019** (63 v. 61–62 in 2009; 79 v. 62 in 2018); **Saturn took the lead in October 2019** (82) – keys of 2009/2011 papers that said Jupiter are now outdated.
[0:0:14] > Statement angle (CGPSC 2020): "Mercury hottest" ✗ (Venus); "Ganymede satellite of Saturn" ✗ (Jupiter); "Neptune has methane / rings" ✓; "Phobos and Deimos – Mars" ✓ → "3 and 4".
[0:0:15] ~~ Sources: Rocket General Geography #1 p.2-4; NextGen Indian Geography - APPSC Exams 01 p.9-13; PMF Physical Geography 01 p.31-32; earthsky.org / space.com (moon counts, March 2026); app notes 4:0 sub 5-6
[0:0:16] - **Jupiter's rings:** faint rings of fine **dust (silicate/rocky particles)** knocked off its small inner moons – not gases.
[0:0:17] - **Triton** = largest moon of **Neptune** (retrograde orbit); match: Titan–Saturn, Ganymede–Jupiter, Phobos–Mars, Triton–Neptune.
[0:0:18] - **NGTS-4b** ('forbidden planet', 2019): a **Neptune-sized** exoplanet found in the **'Neptunian desert'** close to its star, where such planets were thought unable to survive.
## SUB 1 | Earth, Moon and Eclipses
[0:1:0] # Earth's unique features
[0:1:1] - **Goldilocks (habitable) zone:** "just right" for liquid water; Earth lies in it (APPSC 2019). Earth-like **K2-18b**: possible biomarker molecule.
[0:1:2] TABLE head: Sphere | Evolution
  [0:1:2.0] Lithosphere | Differentiation: heavy iron sank to the core, light material formed the crust
  [0:1:2.1] Atmosphere | H-He lost to the solar wind → degassing (water vapour, N₂, CO₂) → photosynthesis added oxygen
  [0:1:2.2] Hydrosphere | Degassed vapour condensed → rain → oceans
  [0:1:2.3] Magnetic field | Geodynamo of the liquid-iron outer core; 2026 study: some reversals lasted ~70,000 years
[0:1:3] # Earth's orbit
[0:1:4] TABLE head: Basis | Perihelion | Aphelion
  [0:1:4.0] Date | ~3 January | ~4 July
  [0:1:4.1] Distance | ~147 million km | ~152 million km
  [0:1:4.2] Speed | Fastest | Slowest
  [0:1:4.3] N. Hemisphere | Winter | Summer
[0:1:5] - **Kepler's Second Law** (equal areas in equal times) explains the speed change. Seasons come from **revolution + axial tilt**, not distance.
[0:1:6] # The Moon
[0:1:7] - Mean distance **384,400 km**; gravity **1/6** of Earth's; rotation = revolution = **27 d 7 h 43 min** (same face); synodic (lunar) month **29.5 days**.
[0:1:8] - **Supermoon:** full moon when the Moon is **nearest the Earth (perigee)**, with Sun, Earth and Moon in line → bigger, brighter, higher tides. **Micromoon:** full moon at **apogee**, 6–7% smaller.
[0:1:9] - **Blue moon:** 2nd full moon in a calendar month; **blue micromoon** in 2026. Half moon: angle **90°**.
[0:1:10] TABLE head: Basis | Solar eclipse | Lunar eclipse
  [0:1:10.0] Position | Moon between Sun and Earth | Earth between Sun and Moon (Earth's shadow on the Moon)
  [0:1:10.1] Phase | New moon (Amavasya) | Full moon
  [0:1:10.2] Feature | Corona, diamond ring | Reddish "blood moon"
[0:1:11] - Longest total solar eclipse of the 21st century: **22 July 2009**; longest annular eclipse of the millennium: **15 January 2010**.
[0:1:12] - **Apollo 11** (20 July 1969): Armstrong and Aldrin walked, **Collins** orbited. **Blue Marble** photo: Apollo 17.
[0:1:13] ~~ Sources: Rocket General Geography #1 p.5-9; NextGen Indian Geography - APPSC Exams 01 p.2-3; app notes 4:0 sub 7
[0:1:14] - **Moon's core:** small, iron-rich and **solid** at the centre (solid inner core ~500 km across, Briaud et al., *Nature* 2023), unlike the Earth's **liquid outer core**.
[0:1:15] - **Sea of Tranquility** (Mare Tranquillitatis): a dark lava plain (mare) on the **Moon**; Apollo 11 landed there (1969).
[0:1:16] - **Diamond ring:** beads of sunlight shining through valleys on the Moon's rugged edge just **before and after totality**; UPSC 1996 key (PMF): seen **only along the peripheral regions of the totality trail**.
[0:1:17] - **TOI 700 e** (NASA TESS, January 2023): planet about **95% of Earth's size** in the **habitable zone** of the red dwarf TOI 700.
[0:1:18] - **Last man on the Moon:** **Eugene Cernan**, Apollo 17 (December 1972); 12 men in all walked on it (Artemis II, 2026, was a flyby, no landing).
[0:1:19] - **Escape velocity:** Earth **11.2 km/s** (Moon ~2.4 km/s); orbital velocity near Earth ~7.9 km/s.
[0:1:20] - **21 August 2017:** total solar eclipse across **North America** (the USA coast to coast – "Great American Eclipse").
[0:1:21] - **Totality only in a narrow path:** the **Moon's shadow (umbra) on the Earth is small**, so a total solar eclipse is seen only along a narrow **path of totality**.
[0:1:22] - **No air on the Moon:** there is **no atmosphere / no oxygen**, so nothing can burn there, sound does not travel and the sky is black.
## SUB 2 | Distance Units, Light Travel and the Universe
[0:2:0] # Units of astronomical distance
[0:2:1] TABLE head: Unit | Meaning | Value
  [0:2:1.0] Astronomical unit (AU) | Mean Earth–Sun distance | ~149.6 million km (149.598 × 10⁶ km)
  [0:2:1.1] Light year | Distance light travels in one year | 9.461 × 10¹² km (~9.46 trillion km)
  [0:2:1.2] Parsec | Largest of the three | 3.26 light years
[0:2:2] - **Light year = distance, not time** (asked 9 times); it is the unit for **distances of stars**. Sun–Earth = **1 AU**.
[0:2:3] - **Why light years:** the **speed of light is constant** (~3 × 10⁵ km/s) (UPSC 2021).
[0:2:4] - **"4.3 light years away"** (Alpha Centauri) = its light takes **4.3 years** to reach Earth.
[0:2:5] - **Sunlight reaches Earth in 8 min 19 s** (~**500 seconds**, "about 8 minutes").
[0:2:6] - **Order of size:** AU < light year < parsec. **Not** distance units: mile/fathom are not used for stars; "longsec" is not a unit at all.
[0:2:7] - **Cepheids** (stars that brighten and dim) are used to measure astronomical distances.
[0:2:8] # The universe
[0:2:9] - **Cosmology** = study of the origin, evolution and structure of the **universe**.
[0:2:10] - **Age of the universe: 13.8 billion years** (APPSC 2019). **Age of the Earth: ~4.54 billion years** (~4.6 billion for the solar system).
[0:2:11] - **Most abundant element** in the universe and in the Sun: **hydrogen**, then **helium** (Sun ~74% H, ~24% He).
[0:2:12] - **Milky Way** = **barred spiral galaxy**; the Sun lies in the **Orion Arm**, ~26,000 light years from the centre, and takes **~225–250 million years (~25 crore years)** to go once round the galactic centre (a "galactic year").
[0:2:13] - **Constellations:** **88** recognised by the **IAU** (founded 1919, Paris).
[0:2:14] - News terms **event horizon, singularity, string theory, Standard Model** belong to the observation and understanding of the **universe** (UPSC 2017).
[0:2:15] ~~ Sources: Rocket General Geography #1 p.5-6; PMF Physical Geography 01 p.8, p.19; NextGen Indian Geography - APPSC Exams 01 p.7; app notes 4:0 sub 0
[0:2:16] - **Cosmic dust:** tiny solid particles of matter spread throughout space (interplanetary and interstellar dust).
[0:2:17] - **Moonlight reaches Earth in ~1.3 seconds** (384,400 km ÷ 3 × 10⁵ km/s).
[0:2:18] - **Intergalactic space** is almost a perfect **vacuum** (only a few atoms of hot, thin gas per cubic metre).
## SUB 3 | Planets – Order, Distance, Size and Classification
[0:3:0] # Order and data
[0:3:1] TABLE head: Planet | Distance from Sun (million km) | Diameter (km) | Revolution | Density (g/cm³)
  [0:3:1.0] Mercury | 57.9 | 4,879 | 88 days | 5.4
  [0:3:1.1] Venus | 108.2 | 12,104 | 224.7 days | 5.2
  [0:3:1.2] Earth | 149.6 | 12,756 | 365.25 days | 5.5
  [0:3:1.3] Mars | 228 | 6,792 | 687 days | 3.9
  [0:3:1.4] Jupiter | 778.5 | 142,984 | 11.86 years | 1.3
  [0:3:1.5] Saturn | 1,432 | 120,536 | 29.4 years | 0.7
  [0:3:1.6] Uranus | 2,867 | 51,118 | 84.07 years | 1.3
  [0:3:1.7] Neptune | 4,515 | 49,528 | 164.9 years | 1.6
[0:3:2] - **Order from the Sun:** Mercury → Venus → **Earth** → Mars → **Jupiter → Saturn** → Uranus → Neptune (Pluto, dwarf, beyond).
[0:3:3]   - Between Mars and Uranus: **Jupiter and Saturn** (UPSC 2008); Earth lies between **Venus and Mars**; between the Sun and Earth: **Mercury and Venus**.
[0:3:4] - **Size (decreasing):** **Jupiter > Saturn > Uranus > Neptune > Earth > Venus > Mars > Mercury**; Venus is the only listed planet just **smaller than Earth**.
[0:3:5] - **Number of planets:** **8** since the IAU decision of **August 2006** (Pluto dropped); **9** before 2006.
[0:3:6] - **Period rises with distance:** a smaller orbit gives a **shorter year** (if the Sun–Earth distance fell by a quarter, the year would be shorter). Tropical year: **365 d 6 h 9 min 9.51 s**.
[0:3:7] # Classification
[0:3:8] - **Inner / terrestrial planets:** **Mercury, Venus, Earth, Mars** – rocky, dense, solid surface; lie **between the Sun and the asteroid belt**.
[0:3:9] - **Outer / Jovian planets:** **Jupiter, Saturn** (**gas giants**), **Uranus, Neptune** (**ice giants**) – beyond the asteroid belt, thick H-He atmospheres.
[0:3:10] - **Planets are non-luminous** – they **shine by reflected sunlight**; stars twinkle, planets do not. The Sun's gravity gives the **centripetal force** that keeps planets in orbit.
[0:3:11] ~~ Sources: Rocket General Geography #1 p.2-4; NextGen Indian Geography - APPSC Exams 01 p.8-9; app notes 4:0 sub 0, 5
## SUB 4 | Stars – Nearest Star, Colour, Brightness, Twinkling and Constellations
[0:4:0] # Nearest stars
[0:4:1] - **Nearest star to Earth: the Sun**; next: **Proxima Centauri** (4.2 ly), part of the **Alpha Centauri** system (4.37 ly).
[0:4:2] - **Sirius** (Dog Star, in Canis Major) = **brightest star** of the night sky; found by following the line of **Orion's belt** – as the Pole Star is found with Ursa Major.
[0:4:3] # Colour, brightness, twinkling
[0:4:4] - **Colour of a star = its surface temperature** (asked 3 times): **blue** (hottest, 50,000–28,000 K) → white → **yellow** (Sun, 6,000–4,900 K) → orange → **red** (coolest).
[0:4:5]   - Decreasing temperature: **Blue, White, Yellow, Orange, Red**.
[0:4:6] - **Apparent brightness** depends on a star's **size, temperature and distance from the Earth**.
[0:4:7] - **Magnitude:** lower number = brighter (logarithmic); **absolute magnitude** = brightness at **10 parsecs**.
[0:4:8] - **Twinkling:** starlight passing through the atmosphere is refracted by air of **fluctuating density / temperature** (changing **refractive index**); stars twinkle because they are **very distant** point sources; planets do not.
[0:4:9] - **In space** (no atmosphere): the **sky is black** and **stars do not twinkle**.
[0:4:10] - Hubble's first direct image of the surface of another star: **Betelgeuse** (red supergiant in Orion).
[0:4:11] # Constellations and the Pole Star
[0:4:12] - **Constellation** = a pattern of stars in the sky; its stars are **not equidistant** from Earth – they only look grouped.
[0:4:13] - **Saptarishi** (Big Dipper, part of **Ursa Major**) points to the **Pole Star** and so gives the direction of the pole.
[0:4:14] - **Polaris** (**Ursa Minor**) lies within 1° of the north celestial pole → always shows **north**; walking **east**, keep the Pole Star on your **left**. **Vega** will be pole star in ~**12,000 years**.
[0:4:15] - **Nebulae** (gas and dust clouds where stars form) are **not members of the solar system**; asteroids, comets and planets are.
[0:4:16] ~~ Sources: PMF Physical Geography 01 p.8, p.18, p.37; NextGen Indian Geography - APPSC Exams 01 p.7-8; app notes 4:0 sub 3
## SUB 5 | Stars – Life Cycle, Black Holes and the Chandrasekhar Limit
[0:5:0] # Life cycle
[0:5:1] - **Nebula** → **protostar** → **main sequence**; then low/medium mass (Sun): **red giant** → planetary nebula → **white dwarf**; massive: **supergiant** → **supernova** → **neutron star** or **black hole**.
[0:5:2] - **Supernova** = the explosive death of a star (**a dying star**).
[0:5:3] TABLE head: Term | Meaning
  [0:5:3.0] Nebulae | Giant clouds of dust and gas
  [0:5:3.1] Cepheids | Stars that brighten and dim periodically
  [0:5:3.2] Pulsars | Spinning neutron stars formed when massive stars run out of fuel and collapse
  [0:5:3.3] Black hole | Remains of a collapsed massive star; no light escapes
[0:5:4] - UPSC 2023 pairs: Cepheids–"clouds of dust" ✗, Nebulae–"brighten and dim" ✗, Pulsars ✓ → **only one** correct.
[0:5:5] # Black holes
[0:5:6] - **Black hole:** gravity so strong (very **high density**) that **not even light escapes** → it **cannot be seen by a telescope** (A-R: both true, R explains A).
[0:5:7] - A black hole is a **collapsed star**, a celestial phenomenon of stars; Pulsar, Black Hole, Quasar are in space – **Brittle Star** is a sea animal (echinoderm).
[0:5:8] # Chandrasekhar
[0:5:9] - **S. Chandrasekhar** gave black-hole/white-dwarf theory (paper to the Royal Astronomical Society, London, **11 January 1935**; Nobel 1983).
[0:5:10] - **Chandrasekhar limit ≈ 1.44 solar masses**: stars below it end as **white dwarfs**; above it the core suffers **internal collapse** (neutron star or black hole).
[0:5:11] ~~ Sources: PMF Physical Geography 01 p.9-12; UPSC CSE 2023 pair question; app notes 4:0 sub 2-3
[0:5:12] - **Sun replaced by a black hole of equal mass:** the Earth's **orbit would stay nearly the same** – gravity depends only on mass and distance; the Earth would just go dark and cold.
## SUB 6 | Dwarf Planets, Asteroids, Comets and Meteors
[0:6:0] TABLE head: Basis | Asteroids | Comets
  [0:6:0.0] Made of | Rock and metal | Ice, frozen gases, dust, rock
  [0:6:0.1] Where | Mars–Jupiter belt; largest Ceres (~1,000 km) | Kuiper Belt, Oort Cloud
  [0:6:0.2] Orbit | Less eccentric | Highly elliptical
  [0:6:0.3] Tail | Usually none | Coma and tail, points away from the Sun (radiation pressure + solar wind)
[0:6:1] - **Kuiper Belt** (30–50 AU, beyond Neptune) → **short-period** comets; **Oort Cloud** → **long-period** comets.
[0:6:2] TABLE head: Comet | Fact
  [0:6:2.0] Halley's | Short-period, ~76 yr; last 1986, next 2061
  [0:6:2.1] Hale-Bopp | Long-period, ~2,534 yr
  [0:6:2.2] Shoemaker-Levy 9 | Hit Jupiter, 16–22 July 1994
  [0:6:2.3] 3I/ATLAS | Third interstellar object; JWST found methane (a first)
[0:6:3] - **Meteoroid** = body in space; **meteor** = **shooting star**; **meteorite** = reaches the ground; **bolide** = explodes in the air.
[0:6:4] - **Leonids:** debris of **Comet Tempel-Tuttle**; appear to come from **Leo**; peak ~17–18 November.
[0:6:5] - Match (UPPCS 1992): Planet – **Uranus**; Satellite – **Moon**; Comet – **Halley**; Spacecraft – **Mariner**.
[0:6:6] # Dwarf planets
[0:6:7] - **IAU General Assembly (August 2006, Prague):** a planet must orbit the Sun, be nearly round and **clear its orbit**; Pluto fails → dwarf planet; trigger **Eris**.
[0:6:8] - Dwarf planets: **Ceres** (asteroid belt); **Pluto, Haumea, Makemake, Eris** (Kuiper belt). Pluto: Indian name **Yama**; Eris is not a moon of Jupiter.
[0:6:9] ~~ Sources: Rocket General Geography #1 p.9-10; NextGen Indian Geography - APPSC Exams 01 p.11-13; app notes 4:0 sub 8
## SUB 7 | The Sun – Composition, Layers and Activity
[0:7:0] - **Chief body of the solar system** and the **primary source of energy for the Earth**; **99.8%** of the solar system's mass; diameter **109 × Earth's**.
[0:7:1] - **Composition:** mostly **hydrogen** (~74%), then helium; energy from **nuclear fusion** of H into He in the core (**~15 million °C**).
[0:7:2] - **State:** the interior is **plasma (gas)** – the centre is in the **gaseous state only**, not liquid or solid.
[0:7:3] - **Light of the Sun is white** (all colours together); it looks yellow from Earth because of scattering.
[0:7:4] # Layers (centre outward)
[0:7:5] TABLE head: Layer | Key fact
  [0:7:5.0] Core | Fusion H → He; ~15 million °C
  [0:7:5.1] Photosphere | Bright visible outer surface emitting most radiation; ~5,500–6,000°C (5,778 K)
  [0:7:5.2] Chromosphere | Reddish lower atmosphere (~4,300°C), seen at eclipses
  [0:7:5.3] Corona | Outermost; visible only during a total solar eclipse; over 1 million °C
[0:7:6] # Solar activity
[0:7:7] TABLE head: Phenomenon | Fact
  [0:7:7.0] Sunspots | Cooler dark magnetic patches on the photosphere; the interval between two maxima (~11 years) = cycle of solar activity (solar cycle)
  [0:7:7.1] Solar flares | Bursts from magnetic reconnection; classes A, B, C, M, X (X strongest)
  [0:7:7.2] CMEs | Coronal plasma (not rock), 250–3,000 km/s, reach Earth in 1–3 days
  [0:7:7.3] Aurora | Borealis (north), Australis (south)
[0:7:8] ~~ Sources: Rocket General Geography #1 p.6; PMF Physical Geography 01 p.22-23; app notes 4:0 sub 4
## SUB 8 | Space Missions and Lagrange Points
[0:8:0] TABLE head: Mission | Target / achievement
  [0:8:0.0] Chang'e 4 (China) | First soft landing on the far side of the Moon (3 January 2019)
  [0:8:0.1] Chandrayaan-3 (2023) | India first near the Moon's south pole
  [0:8:0.2] OSIRIS-REx (NASA) | Bennu samples (24 Sep 2023); renamed OSIRIS-APEX for Apophis (2029)
  [0:8:0.3] DART | Kinetic impact on Dimorphos (planetary defence)
  [0:8:0.4] Hera (ESA) | Surveys Didymos-Dimorphos after DART
  [0:8:0.5] Hayabusa2 (Japan) | Ryugu samples (not Bennu)
  [0:8:0.6] Phoenix Mars Lander | Landed 25 May 2008 near Mars's north pole; found ice
  [0:8:0.7] SMILE (ESA-China) | Solar wind hitting Earth's magnetic shield, in X-ray and UV
[0:8:1] - **Aditya-L1:** first Indian solar observatory; **PSLV-C57** (2023) from **SDSC-SHAR, Sriharikota (AP)**; halo orbit at Sun–Earth **L1** (~**1.5 million km**); **VELC** made the first **visible-light** spectroscopy of CMEs.
[0:8:2] - **Mangalyaan** (Mars, not lunar): **PSLV-C25**, Nov 2013; Mars orbit **24 Sep 2014**; India first Asian nation at Mars.
[0:8:3] - **Lagrange points:** **5**; **L4, L5 stable**; L1–L3 unstable.
[0:8:4] - AP angle: SDSC-SHAR's **100th launch** on **29 January 2025**. Himalayan Chandra Telescope, **Hanle**: 25 years in 2026. India's first satellite: **Aryabhata** (1975).
[0:8:5] ~~ Sources: CNSA / Britannica (Chang'e 4, 3 Jan 2019); ISRO; app notes 4:0 sub 9-10
[0:8:6] - **Evolved LISA (eLISA, ESA):** planned gravitational-wave observatory – **three spacecraft in an equilateral triangle** with ~1 million km sides, linked by lasers (UPSC 2020); LISA Pathfinder was only the test craft.
[0:8:7] - **Deep Space Atomic Clock (NASA):** launched into **space** (Earth orbit) on 25 June 2019 to test atomic timekeeping for deep-space navigation.
[0:8:8] - **Rosetta / Philae (ESA):** first landing on a comet, **67P/Churyumov-Gerasimenko**, 12 November 2014 – to study whether comets brought **water and organic matter** to Earth.
[0:8:9] - **Kepler space telescope (NASA, 2009-2018):** searched for **Earth-like (Earth-size) planets** in the habitable zone of other stars by the transit method.
[0:8:10] - **Cassini** (NASA–ESA–ASI) = **spacecraft** that orbited **Saturn** 2004–2017 (ended with a plunge into Saturn); its Huygens probe landed on Titan.
[0:8:11] - **Magellan (NASA):** radar mapper of **Venus** (launched 4 May 1989, orbit from August 1990).
[0:8:12] - **NASA centres:** **Jet Propulsion Laboratory – Pasadena** (California); Johnson Space Center – Houston; Kennedy Space Center – Cape Canaveral.
[0:8:13] - **Google AI + NASA Kepler data (December 2017):** machine learning found **Kepler-90i** (making Kepler-90 the first 8-planet system like ours) and **Kepler-80g**.
[0:8:14] - **Juno (NASA):** Jupiter orbiter (launched 2011, in orbit since 2016) – atmosphere, magnetic field, interior.
## SUB 9 | Origin of the Universe and the Solar System – Theories
[0:9:0] TABLE head: Theory | Proponent | Year
  [0:9:0.0] Geocentric (Earth at the centre) | Ptolemy | 2nd c. AD
  [0:9:0.1] Heliocentric | Copernicus | 1543
  [0:9:0.2] Nebular hypothesis | Immanuel Kant; revised by Laplace | 1755; 1796
  [0:9:0.3] Planetesimal hypothesis | Chamberlin and Moulton | 1904
  [0:9:0.4] Tidal hypothesis | James Jeans and Harold Jeffreys | –
  [0:9:0.5] Nova hypothesis | Hoyle and Lyttleton | –
  [0:9:0.6] Interstellar dust (gas and dust) | Otto Schmidt | 1943
  [0:9:0.7] Big Bang (expanding universe) | Georges Lemaitre | 1927
  [0:9:0.8] Steady State | Hoyle, Bondi and Gold | 1948
[0:9:1] - **Earth from gases and dust particles = O. Schmidt** (asked 4 times: APPSC G2 2008, IAS 1999); not Jeans (tidal) or Hoyle (nova).
[0:9:2] - **Big Bang** explains the **origin of the universe** (not continental drift, the Himalayas or volcanoes).
[0:9:3] - **Evidence for Big Bang / expansion:** **red-shift** of galaxies (**Hubble, 1929**) = **expansion of the universe**, and the **cosmic microwave background** → UPSC 2012 answer "**1 and 2**" (asteroids and supernovae are not evidence).
[0:9:4] - Lemaitre proposed the Big Bang (1927); Hubble only showed expansion (1929). Steady State = **Gold and Bondi** (with Hoyle).
[0:9:5] - **Present view:** planets formed from the same spinning disc; the hot inner disc kept only metals and rocky minerals, ices stayed in the cool outer disc (giant planets).
[0:9:6] ~~ Sources: PMF Physical Geography 01 p.18-20; Rocket General Geography #1 p.10; app notes 4:0 sub 1
## SUB 10 | Astronomers and Discoveries
[0:10:0] TABLE head: Person | Contribution
  [0:10:0.0] Nicolaus Copernicus (16th-c. astronomer) | First to state that the Earth and planets revolve round the Sun (heliocentric model, 1543)
  [0:10:0.1] Johannes Kepler | Planets move in ellipses (first law); equal areas in equal times (second law)
  [0:10:0.2] Galileo | Discovered the four Galilean moons of Jupiter (Io, Europa, Ganymede, Callisto), 1610
  [0:10:0.3] Newton | Laws of gravitation
  [0:10:0.4] William Herschel | Discovered Uranus, 1781
  [0:10:0.5] Johann Galle | First saw Neptune, 1846
  [0:10:0.6] Clyde Tombaugh | Discovered Pluto, 1930
  [0:10:0.7] Aryabhata (476–550 CE) | Earth rotates on its axis; explained eclipses; India's first satellite named after him
[0:10:1] - **⚠ Trap:** Copernicus = heliocentrism (not the spherical Earth: that was Pythagoras/Aristotle); circular orbits were replaced by ellipses only with **Kepler**.
[0:10:2] ~~ Sources: Rocket General Geography #1 p.10; PMF Physical Geography 01 p.20; NextGen Indian Geography - APPSC Exams 01 p.12; app notes 4:0 sub 2

  x: The Big Bang was proposed by Georges Lemaitre (1927); Hubble only showed the expansion of the universe (1929).
[gdb3d6db899ac] sub9
  With which hypothesis of the origin of the Earth is Otto Schmidt associated?
  Tidal hypothesis | Planetesimal hypothesis | *Interstellar dust hypothesis | Steady State theory
  x: Otto Schmidt said the Earth formed from interstellar gas and dust.
[ngeneral-geography-1-9] sub10
  Who discovered Uranus?
  Clyde Tombaugh | Johannes Kepler | *William Herschel | Nicolaus Copernicus
  x: William Herschel found Uranus; Tombaugh found Pluto, Kepler gave the three laws and Copernicus the heliocentric model.
[ngeneral-geography-1-80] sub10
  With the discovery of which of the following is Johann Galle associated?
  Pluto | Uranus | *Neptune | The Galilean moons
  x: Johann Galle first observed Neptune in 1846. Herschel found Uranus, Tombaugh Pluto and Galileo the Galilean moons.
[n6084f63ced8] sub10
  Who among the following is associated with the first mathematically predictive heliocentric model?
  Aryabhata | Ptolemy | *Copernicus | Varahamihira
  x: That model is associated with Copernicus; Aryabhata held that the Earth rotates on its axis and explained eclipses.
[ff41718e3b5a2] sub10
  In which year did Galileo first see the four large moons of Jupiter?
  1781 | 1846 | 1543 | *1610
  x: Galileo discovered the four Galilean moons of Jupiter (Io, Europa, Ganymede, Callisto) in 1610.
[f15f82ded70f1] sub10
  Who discovered Pluto in 1930?
  William Herschel | Johann Galle | *Clyde Tombaugh | Edwin Hubble
  x: Clyde Tombaugh discovered Pluto in 1930; Herschel found Uranus and Galle first saw Neptune.
[f5bd51fb14c33] sub10
  Which ancient Indian astronomer stated that the Earth rotates on its axis and explained eclipses?
  *Aryabhata | Varahamihira | Brahmagupta | Bhaskara
  x: Aryabhata (476–550 CE) held that the Earth rotates on its axis and explained eclipses.

=== NOTES 4:1 | Earth – Shape, Size, Geoid; Latitudes and Longitudes, the Graticule
## SUB 0 | Key Parallels, Meridians and the Countries They Cross
[1:0:0] # The five key lines (asked most: 25 PYQs)
[1:0:1] TABLE head: Line | Value | Passes through | Does NOT pass through (traps)
  [1:0:1.0] Equator | 0° | 13 countries: Ecuador, Colombia, Brazil, São Tomé and Príncipe, Gabon, Rep. of Congo, DR Congo, Uganda, Kenya, Somalia, Maldives (waters), Indonesia, Kiribati | Rwanda, Equatorial Guinea (UPPSC 2020), Mexico, Venezuela, Zambia, Sudan, Ethiopia, Malaysia, Cameroon, Costa Rica
  [1:0:1.1] Tropic of Cancer | 23½°N | Mexico, Bahamas, Western Sahara, Mauritania, Mali, Algeria, Niger, Libya, Egypt, Saudi Arabia, UAE, Oman, India, Bangladesh, Myanmar, China, Taiwan | Iran (UPPSC 2005)
  [1:0:1.2] Tropic of Capricorn | 23½°S | Chile, Argentina, Paraguay, Brazil, Namibia, Botswana, South Africa, Mozambique, Madagascar, Australia | Bolivia (asked 3 times)
  [1:0:1.3] Arctic Circle | 66½°N | Norway, Sweden, Finland, Russia, USA (Alaska), Canada, Greenland | Iceland – mainland lies just south of it (only tiny Grímsey island is touched)
  [1:0:1.4] Prime Meridian | 0° | UK (Greenwich), France, Spain, Algeria, Mali, Burkina Faso, Togo, Ghana | Niger (on the Tropic of Cancer instead), Nigeria, Portugal
[1:0:2] - **Equator groups (UPSC 2006, asked twice):** **Colombia, Kenya and Indonesia** ✓; Brazil–Zambia–Malaysia ✗, Brazil–Sudan–Malaysia ✗, Venezuela–Ethiopia–Indonesia ✗.
[1:0:3] - **Continents:** **Africa** is the only continent crossed by the **Equator and both Tropics**; South America has the Equator and Capricorn; Asia the Equator and Cancer.
[1:0:4] - **0° latitude, 0° longitude** = **Gulf of Guinea, Atlantic Ocean** (south of Ghana) – not Ghana, not Greenwich (asked 3 times).
[1:0:5] - **49th Parallel** = long stretch of the **USA–Canada** boundary.
[1:0:6] - **Torrid zone** = between the two Tropics; temperate zones 23½°–66½°; frigid zones beyond the polar circles (**Antarctic Circle 66½°S**). The **International Date Line** follows roughly **180°**, not 0° (SSC 2012: II and III wrong).
[1:0:7] - **Arctic** region lies around the **North Pole**, **Antarctica** around the **South Pole**.
[1:0:8] - **Tropics are 47° apart**; on the **December solstice** the noon Sun at the Tropic of Cancer stands at **90° − 47° = 43°** (UPPSC 1997).
[1:0:9] - **Midnight sun:** seen beyond the polar circles up to the **poles** (North Pole ✓, Equator ✗).
[1:0:10] - **Longest north–south (latitudinal) extent:** **Chile** (~39° of latitude, ~4,300 km; CIA World Factbook), then Brazil (~38–39°); **not China** (~35° on the mainland).
[1:0:11] > NOT-angle: the trap countries in the last column are the answers to "does not pass through".
[1:0:12] ~~ Sources: NextGen Rocket General Geography #2 p.16; PMF Physical Geography 01; CIA World Factbook (Chile); app notes 4:1 sub 3
## SUB 1 | Shape and Size of the Earth – Geoid, Gravity, Dimensions
[1:1:0] # Shape
[1:1:1] - **Geoid** = "earth-shaped"; the Earth is an **oblate spheroid** – **flattened at the poles, bulging at the Equator**.
[1:1:2] - **Cause:** **centrifugal force of rotation**, maximum at the Equator (the faster-moving equatorial belt) – not the Sun's pull or stronger sunlight (CDS 2010: "only I").
[1:1:3] - **Equatorial bulge:** equatorial radius exceeds polar radius by **~21 km** (diameters by ~43 km) (CDS 2008, NDA 2016).
[1:1:4] - **Gravity and weight:** **g is greater at the poles** than at the Equator; **weight is least at the Equator, greatest at the poles** (poles are ~21 km nearer the centre + no centrifugal force). Moving to **higher latitude, weight increases** (IAS 2003 A-R: A false, R true).
[1:1:5] - Surface point nearest the Earth's centre: the **poles** (Arctic Ocean floor near the North Pole).
[1:1:6] - **Proofs of sphericity:** circular shadow of the Earth on the Moon in a **lunar eclipse**; ship's mast seen first; circumnavigation; satellite images.
[1:1:7] - **First to say the Earth is spherical:** **Pythagoras** (c. 6th century BC) proposed it; **Aristotle** (4th century BC, *On the Heavens*) gave the first proofs – Indian books and keys say **Aristotle** when Pythagoras is not offered (IAS 2001). Not Copernicus (heliocentric model), Ptolemy, Newton (polar flattening) or Columbus.
[1:1:8] - **Stars rise perpendicular to the horizon** for an observer on the **Equator** (celestial poles lie on the horizon there); at the poles stars circle parallel to the horizon (UPSC 2001, asked twice).
[1:1:9] # Dimensions
[1:1:10] TABLE head: Measure | Value
  [1:1:10.0] Equatorial diameter | 12,756 km (~12,800 km)
  [1:1:10.1] Polar diameter | 12,714 km (~43 km less)
  [1:1:10.2] Mean radius | 6,371 km
  [1:1:10.3] Equatorial circumference (length of the Equator) | ~40,075 km (~40,000 km)
  [1:1:10.4] Water : land | 71 : 29 – about two-thirds of the surface is water
[1:1:11] ~~ Sources: NextGen Rocket General Geography #1 p.4, p.7; PMF Physical Geography 01; Wikipedia "Equatorial bulge" (WGS-84: radii differ by 21.38 km); app notes 4:1 sub 0-1
[1:1:12] - **Level surface:** a surface **parallel to the mean spheroidal surface** of the Earth (perpendicular to the plumb line everywhere).
## SUB 2 | The Graticule – Latitude, Longitude, Great Circles
[1:2:0] # Latitude and longitude
[1:2:1] - **Latitude** = angular distance of a point **north or south of the Equator**, measured **from the centre of the Earth** (NDA 2011); 0°–90°.
[1:2:2] - **Parallels of latitude run east–west** (APPSC G1); **meridians run north–south**, pole to pole; longitude 0°–180° E or W.
[1:2:3] - Parallels at 1°: **179** (89 N + 89 S + Equator; the poles are points). Meridians: **360**.
[1:2:4] - 1° of latitude ≈ **111 km** everywhere; 1° of longitude: 111 km at the Equator → **55.5 km at 60°** → 0 at the poles.
[1:2:5] - **Antipode** of (x°N, y°E) = (x°S, (180 − y)°W): 20°N 80°E → **20°S 100°W**.
[1:2:6] # Great circles
[1:2:7] - A **great circle** divides the globe into **two equal halves** (its plane passes through the centre): the **Equator** (the only great-circle parallel) and every meridian with its opposite – **0° + 180°** form one (UPPSC 2000).
[1:2:8] - **60°N latitude is NOT a great circle** (all parallels except the Equator are small circles); 60°E longitude is half of a great circle.
[1:2:9] - NDA 2009 A-R: Equator is a great circle ✓; R (its plane divides the Earth into two equal halves) ✓ and explains A.
[1:2:10] # What varies with latitude (CDS 2017: all three true)
[1:2:11] - **Rainfall, temperature and vegetation** vary with latitude; the **difference between longest and shortest day increases with latitude**.
[1:2:12] - **Longitude does not affect insolation** (latitude, atmospheric transparency, distance from sea do).
[1:2:13] # Places by longitude / latitude
[1:2:14] - **West to east:** **Lisbon (9°W) → London (0°) → Frankfurt (8.7°E) → Beirut (35.5°E)** (MPPSC 2008).
[1:2:15] - **Nearest the Equator:** **Singapore (1°17'N)** < Jakarta (6°12'S) < Colombo (6°56'N) < Manila (14°35'N) (IAS 2008).
[1:2:16] ~~ Sources: NextGen Rocket General Geography #2; PMF Physical Geography 01; NCERT Class 6 "The Earth: Our Habitat" Ch. 2; app notes 4:1 sub 2
[1:2:17] - **Shapes on the globe:** a meridian of longitude = **semicircle**; a pole = **point (dot)**; the Equator (prime latitude) = **circle**; the axis = **straight line**.
## SUB 3 | Pioneers, Maps and Projections
[1:3:0] TABLE head: Who / what | Fact
  [1:3:0.0] Eratosthenes | Father of Geography; coined "Geography"; first correct estimate of the Earth's circumference
  [1:3:0.1] Hipparchus | First to divide the circle into 360°
  [1:3:0.2] Anaximander | First Greek world map
  [1:3:0.3] Ptolemy | First used latitude and longitude (*Geographia*)
  [1:3:0.4] Amundsen | First at the South Pole, 1911
  [1:3:0.5] International Meridian Conference, 1884 | Fixed the Prime Meridian at Greenwich
  [1:3:0.6] Mercator, 1569 | Right-angled graticule, straight rhumb lines, polar areas greatly enlarged
[1:3:1] - **Survey of India** (1767, Dehradun) prepares **topographical maps**; Geological Survey of India – geological maps; NATMO – thematic maps.
[1:3:2] - **"Correct the Map":** UNGA non-binding resolution of **4 September 2026** (164–1, **US against**), led by Togo and the **African Union**, promotes the **Equal Earth** projection (2018) over Mercator.
[1:3:3] - **EOS-05**: ISRO Earth-imaging satellite meant for **geosynchronous orbit (~36,000 km)**.
[1:3:4] - **Earth Day: 22 April** (first observed 1970).
[1:3:5] ~~ Sources: NextGen Rocket General Geography #1 p.7; surveyofindia.gov.in; un.org/osaa "Correct the Map" (Sep 2026); app notes 4:1 sub 4
[1:3:6] - **Contours:** values **decreasing towards the centre** (1300 → 1280 → 1260) show a **depression**; values rising towards the centre show a hill.
[1:3:7] - **Graphs and diagrams** (graphic representation) make **comparison of variables easy** at a glance.
## SUB 4 | India and Andhra Pradesh on the Graticule
[1:4:0] # India
[1:4:1] TABLE head: Extreme | Point
  [1:4:1.0] North | Indira Col, Karakoram (Ladakh)
  [1:4:1.1] South (mainland) | Kanyakumari (Cape Comorin), 8°4'N
  [1:4:1.2] South (overall) | Indira Point (6°45'N), Great Nicobar – nearest the Equator
  [1:4:1.3] East | Kibithu, Arunachal Pradesh (97°25'E)
  [1:4:1.4] West | Guhar Moti, Kutch, Gujarat (68°7'E)
[1:4:2] - India: **8°4'N – 37°6'N**, **68°7'E – 97°25'E**; area **32,87,263 sq km**; **3,214 km** N–S, **2,933 km** E–W.
[1:4:3] - **Tropic of Cancer** crosses **8 states:** Gujarat, Rajasthan, MP, Chhattisgarh, Jharkhand, West Bengal, Tripura, Mizoram (**not Odisha**).
[1:4:4] - **24°N** crosses the most states (CAPF 2019; ~9: Gujarat, Rajasthan, MP, UP, Chhattisgarh, Jharkhand, WB, Tripura, Mizoram/Manipur) – more than 20°, 22° or 26°N.
[1:4:5] - **Longitude nearest Delhi (77.2°E):** **Bengaluru (77.6°E)**; Hyderabad 78.5°, Nagpur 79.1°, Pune 73.9°E.
[1:4:6] - **70°E** passes through **Jaisalmer** district (western Rajasthan); Jodhpur, Nagaur, Dholpur lie east of it.
[1:4:7] - North to south: Srinagar > Bhopal > Visakhapatnam > Hyderabad > Chennai > Kanyakumari.
[1:4:8] # Andhra Pradesh
[1:4:9] TABLE head: Item | Fact
  [1:4:9.0] Latitude | 12°41'N – 19°07'N (wholly between the Equator and the Tropic of Cancer; tropical Aw)
  [1:4:9.1] Longitude | 77°E – 84°40'E (older/undivided figures: 76°45'E – 84°46'E)
  [1:4:9.2] Area | 1,62,970 sq km = 4.96% of India; 7th largest state (NextGen: 8th – counted J&K before 2019)
  [1:4:9.3] Coastline | 974 km, 2nd longest (traditional) → 1,053.07 km, 3rd after Gujarat and Tamil Nadu (re-measurement, MoPSW circular 29 Apr 2025)
  [1:4:9.4] Districts | 13 (2014) → 26 (April 2022) → 28 (as of 2026)
[1:4:10] - AP's northern limit is **19°07'N, not 22°N**. Neighbours: Odisha, Chhattisgarh (north), Telangana (west), Karnataka (south-west), Tamil Nadu (south).
[1:4:11] - **IST meridian 82½°E** passes through **Kakinada** district.
[1:4:12] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Geography - APPSC Exams 04 p.8; NextGen AP Geography - APPSC Exams 01 p.2-7, 03 p.20; NextGen Rocket AP Geography #12 p.26, #24 p.49; MoES Lok Sabha reply 4 Dec 2025 (coastline 11,098.81 km); app notes 4:1 sub 5

[n177de5ab099] sub0
  Where does the point at 0° latitude and 0° longitude lie?
  Arabian Sea near the Tropic of Cancer | Pacific Ocean near the Equator | *Gulf of Guinea in the Atlantic Ocean | Indian Ocean near the Prime Meridian
  x: The intersection of the Equator and the Prime Meridian, represented by 0° latitude and 0° longitude, lies in the Gulf of Guinea in the Atlantic Ocean.
[n9c7ac7e6435] sub0
  Which one of the following countries is NOT traversed by the Equator?
  Kenya | Colombia | Indonesia | *Rwanda
  x: The Equator passes through Kenya, Colombia and Indonesia. Rwanda is the commonly tested trap (UPPSC 2020): it lies just south of the Equator and is not crossed by it, as is Equatorial Guinea.
[naa7c94d924c] sub0
  Which one of the following countries is NOT traversed by the Prime Meridian?
  *Niger | Burkina Faso | Togo | Ghana
  x: The Prime Meridian passes through the United Kingdom, France, Spain, Algeria, Mali, Burkina Faso, Togo and Ghana. Niger is not traversed by it.
[ngeneral-geography-3-43] sub0
  Which of the following is NOT correct?
  The Tropics of Cancer and Capricorn are 47° apart | The Tropic of Cancer is 23½° from the Equator | The Arctic Circle lies at 66½°N | *The noon altitude of the Sun at the Tropic of Cancer at the December solstice is 47°
  x: At the December solstice the noon Sun at the Tropic of Cancer stands at 90° − 47° = 43°, not 47°.
[ngeneral-geography-2-39] sub0
  Which of the following pairs is INCORRECTLY matched?
  Tropic of Cancer – 23.5°N | *Arctic Circle – 23.5°N | Equator – 0° latitude | Tropic of Capricorn – 23.5°S
  x: The Arctic Circle is at 66.5°N; 23.5°N is the Tropic of Cancer. The other three pairs are correct.
[fadddbcf700a9] sub0
  Which of the following countries lies on the Tropic of Cancer?
  Ghana | *Niger | Kenya | Chile
  x: Niger lies on the Tropic of Cancer, not on the Prime Meridian.
[fa47e6cf49e1c] sub0
  Which of the following countries is NOT crossed by the Arctic Circle?
  Norway | Finland | *Iceland | Sweden
  x: Iceland lies just south of the Arctic Circle, which crosses Norway, Sweden, Finland, Russia and Canada.
[fe2482ca7634c] sub0
  The Tropic of Cancer and the Tropic of Capricorn are how many degrees apart?
  23.5° | *47° | 43° | 66.5°
  x: Each Tropic lies at 23.5° from the Equator, so they are 47° apart.
[f7cc20ee82864] sub0
  On the December solstice, what is the noon Sun's altitude at the Tropic of Cancer?
  *43° | 47° | 66.5° | 23.5°
  x: The Sun is overhead at Capricorn, 47° away, so its noon altitude at Cancer is 90° − 47° = 43°.
[fc0365d879fc8] sub0
  Which of the following pairs (line – country it crosses) is INCORRECTLY matched?
  Equator – Kenya | Tropic of Capricorn – Australia | Arctic Circle – Finland | *Prime Meridian – Niger
  x: Niger lies on the Tropic of Cancer; the Prime Meridian passes through Ghana, Togo, Burkina Faso and Mali but not Niger.
[g48236bce0a1a] sub0

  Egypt | Mexico | India | *Australia
  x: The Tropic of Capricorn (23.5°S) passes through Chile, Brazil, South Africa and Australia; the others lie on the Tropic of Cancer.

  What does the term 'geoid' mean?
  Sphere-shaped | Egg-shaped | Flat-disc shaped | *Earth-shaped
  x: Geoid means 'earth-shaped'; the Earth is an oblate spheroid, flattened at the poles and bulging at the Equator.
[n089a032c499] sub1
  Which of the following is evidence of the spherical shape of the Earth?
  Occurrence of tides | *The Earth's circular shadow on the Moon during a lunar eclipse | Twinkling of stars | Equal day and night at the equinoxes
  x: The circular lunar-eclipse shadow, like the mast-first view of ships, circumnavigation and satellite images, shows the Earth is spherical.
[ngeneral-geography-1-13] sub1
  Where is the weight of an object on the Earth's surface maximum?
  Equator | Tropic of Cancer | Arctic Circle | *Poles
  x: Rotation and the equatorial bulge reduce effective gravity at the Equator, so weight is least there and greatest at the poles.
[fbd8d048bb40c] sub1
  What is the Earth's shape, flattened at the poles and bulging at the Equator, called?
  Prolate spheroid | Perfect sphere | *Oblate spheroid | Flat disc
  x: The Earth is an oblate spheroid, flattened at the poles and bulging at the Equator.
[f5ee2f43fa4d7] sub1
  Where does the point on the Earth's surface nearest to its centre lie?
  Equator | *Poles | Tropic of Cancer | Mount Everest
  x: Because of polar flattening, the poles (the Arctic Ocean floor near the North Pole) are nearest the centre.
[n69bf01559c3] sub1
  What is the approximate ratio of water-covered area to land area on the Earth's surface?
  80:20 | 50:50 | 29:71 | *71:29
  x: Approximately 71% of the Earth's surface is covered by water and 29% is land.
[ngeneral-geography-1-38] sub1
  What is the approximate equatorial diameter of the Earth?
  12,656 km | 12,714 km | *12,756 km | 12,856 km
  x: The Earth's equatorial diameter is about 12,756 km, while the polar diameter is about 12,714 km. The equatorial circumference is about 40,075 km.
[nc9ee24f1ab9] sub1
  What is the approximate mean radius of the Earth?
  6,271 km | *6,371 km | 6,471 km | 6,571 km
  x: Earth's mean radius is about 6,371 km; the equatorial diameter exceeds the polar diameter by about 43 km.
[f464c7d543f94] sub1
  What is the approximate polar diameter of the Earth?
  *12,714 km | 12,756 km | 12,800 km | 6,371 km
  x: The polar diameter is about 12,714 km, about 43 km less than the equatorial diameter of 12,756 km.
[f1bd8725d0dec] sub1
  By about how much is the Earth's polar diameter shorter than its equatorial diameter?
  21 km | 111 km | 430 km | *43 km
  x: The polar diameter (12,714 km) is about 43 km less than the equatorial (12,756 km).
[f7c9933adbecd] sub1
  What is the approximate equatorial circumference of the Earth?
  12,756 km | 36,000 km | *40,075 km | 44,075 km
  x: The Earth's circumference at the Equator is about 40,075 km.
[ncf52f2623b1] sub2
  How many parallels of latitude are conventionally drawn on the globe at 1° interval?
  181 | *179 | 180 | 360
  x: There are 89 parallels in each hemisphere plus the Equator, making 179 (the poles are points); there are 360 meridians.
[ne0e472ec860] sub2
  Which one of the following is the only great-circle parallel on the Earth?
  Tropic of Cancer | *Equator | Tropic of Capricorn | Arctic Circle
  x: The Equator is the only parallel whose plane passes through the centre of the Earth and is therefore a great circle. All other parallels are small circles.
[nbbcaa5f3c93] sub2
  What is the antipode of the point 20°N, 80°E?
  80°S, 20°W | *20°S, 100°W | 20°S, 80°W | 20°N, 100°W
  x: To find the antipode, change north to south and subtract the longitude from 180°. Thus, the antipode of 20°N, 80°E is 20°S, 100°W.
[n4dcbf55e1b4] sub2
  About what distance does one degree of longitude at 60° latitude represent?
  27.7 km | *55.5 km | 83.3 km | 111 km
  x: Because meridians converge, one degree of longitude is about 111 km at the Equator, 55.5 km at 60° and zero at the poles.
[f481832c3e84d] sub2
  Which of the following is NOT a valid latitude?
  66.5°N | *95°N | 89°S | 23.5°S
  x: Latitudes range from 0° to 90° north or south, so 95°N cannot exist.
[f6af5b0b7dfc4] sub2
  About what distance does one degree of latitude represent?
  55.5 km | 69 km | 180 km | *111 km
  x: One degree of latitude is about 111 km everywhere.
[fc4221b80ed83] sub2
  What is the antipode of the point 30°N, 60°E?
  30°S, 60°W | 60°S, 120°W | *30°S, 120°W | 30°N, 120°W
  x: The antipode of (x°N, y°E) is (x°S, (180 − y)°W), so 30°N 60°E becomes 30°S 120°W.
[g6d7278a3462b] sub2
  In which direction do meridians of longitude run?
  East to west | *North to south | Parallel to the Equator | From the Tropic of Cancer to the Equator
  x: Meridians run north to south from pole to pole; parallels of latitude run east to west.
[g1e1593a4781e] sub2

  *1 only | 2 only | Both 1 and 2 | Neither 1 nor 2
  x: Latitudes run east–west and range from 0° to 90°; longitudes range from 0° to 180°.

  How many meridians of longitude are drawn on the globe at 1° interval?
  179 | 180 | *360 | 361
  x: There are 360 meridians at 1° interval (180 east and 180 west, with 0° and 180° counted once).

  179°E | 90°W | *195°E | 0°
  x: Longitude runs only from 0° to 180° east or west, so 195°E is not valid.

  1851 | *1884 | 1904 | 1947
  x: The Prime Meridian through Greenwich was fixed at the International Meridian Conference in 1884.

=== NOTES 4:2 | Motions of the Earth – Rotation and Revolution; Effects (day and Night, Coriolis Force, Tides)
## SUB 0 | Rotation – Axis, Speed and Effects
[2:0:0] # Rotation basics
[2:0:1] - **Rotation** = the Earth spinning on its **axis** (line through both poles); **revolution** = moving round the Sun.
[2:0:2] - Direction **west to east** – **counter-clockwise** seen from above the North Pole; only **Venus and Uranus** spin the other way.
[2:0:3] - **Axis tilted 23½° (23°30') from the perpendicular to the orbital plane** ("from the ecliptic axis"; 66½° with the orbital plane) – so it is **not at 90°** to the orbit (NDA 2009: both statements ✓).
[2:0:4] - Angular speed **15° per hour** (1° in 4 min). Linear speed **~1,670 km/h at the Equator – the maximum velocity of rotation** – falling to **zero at the poles**.
[2:0:5] TABLE head: Day | Measured against | Length
  [2:0:5.0] Sidereal (one true rotation) | A distant star | 23 h 56 min 4.09 s
  [2:0:5.1] Solar | The Sun | 24 h (~4 min longer, as the Earth also moves along its orbit)
[2:0:6] - Not felt: motion is uniform and the atmosphere moves along with the Earth.
[2:0:7] - **Pole Star** seems fixed because it lies almost **in line with the Earth's axis of rotation**.
[2:0:8] # Effects of rotation (Strahler; NDA 2019: all three)
[2:0:9] - **Day and night** – the diurnal rhythm of daylight and air temperature.
[2:0:10] - **Coriolis deflection** – flow paths of air and water turned sideways.
[2:0:11] - **Movement of the tides** – a place is carried through the tidal bulges twice a day.
[2:0:12] - Also: time difference between longitudes, centrifugal bulge.
[2:0:13] - **Seasons are NOT caused by rotation** (they come from revolution + tilt); day and night are **not** caused by revolution (Jharkhand 2021: both statements false).
[2:0:14] - **No rotation** → no sunrise/sunset and no day-night cycle, but seasons would remain (CDS 2015: I and II).
[2:0:15] - **Faster rotation** → stronger centrifugal effect → **g decreases at the Equator, unchanged at the poles** (NDA 2018).
[2:0:16] # Evidence and pioneers
[2:0:17] - **Aryabhata** (5th century) first explained that the **rotation of the Earth** causes the daily rising and setting of the Sun (*Aryabhatiya*; also his *Aryasiddhanta*).
[2:0:18] - **Foucault pendulum**, Paris **1851**: the plane of swing slowly turns. Other proofs: Coriolis deflection, equatorial bulge, apparent east-to-west motion of the sky.
[2:0:19] # The axis is shifting (UPSC 2025)
[2:0:20] - Studies (ETH Zurich, *Nature Geoscience*/*PNAS* 2024) show the rotation axis drifting and the day lengthening; cause = **mass redistribution**: as **polar ice melts, water moves towards the Equator** (also groundwater loss) – day longer by **~1.33 ms per century** (2000–2020).
[2:0:21] - **Solar flares and CMEs** do bombard the upper atmosphere with energy (true) but **do not shift the axis** → "both II and III correct, only one (III) explains I".
[2:0:22] ~~ Sources: NextGen Rocket General Geography #2 p.11-13, p.18; NextGen Indian Geography - APPSC Exams 01 p.8-9; Strahler, Physical Geography (effects of rotation); app notes 4:2 sub 0-1
[2:0:23] - **Direction by the Pole Star:** it marks **north**, so to walk **east** keep the Pole Star on your **left**.
## SUB 1 | Coriolis and Centrifugal Effects
[2:1:0] - **Coriolis force** = an **apparent force** caused by the **Earth's rotation** (not revolution, tilt, pressure, temperature or magnetism); it shapes winds, ocean currents, cyclones and **large-scale weather patterns**.
[2:1:1] - **Ferrel's law:** moving air and water are deflected to the **right in the Northern Hemisphere** and to the **left in the Southern Hemisphere** – "left in NH" is the trap (APPSC 2022: I and III only).
[2:1:2] - Force = **2Ωv sin(latitude)**: **zero at the Equator**, **maximum at the poles**; grows with **latitude and wind speed**; linked to the **decreasing rotational velocity with increasing latitude** (CDS 2012: all three).
[2:1:3] - Cyclones rarely form within **5° of the Equator**; NH cyclones spin **anticlockwise**, SH clockwise.
[2:1:4] - AP angle: **Cyclone Montha (Oct 2025)** was steered north-northwest to the **Kakinada** coast.
[2:1:5] - **Centrifugal force** of rotation: maximum at the Equator, zero at the poles → **equatorial bulge, polar flattening (oblate spheroid)**; **weight of a body is maximum at the poles**, least at the Equator.
[2:1:6] ~~ Sources: NextGen Rocket General Geography #2 p.12-13; NextGen Indian Geography - APPSC Exams 03 p.12; app notes 4:2 sub 3
## SUB 2 | Tides
[2:2:0] - **Causes (UPSC 2015: a, b and c):** gravitational pull of the **Moon** (main) and the **Sun**, and the **centrifugal force** of the Earth–Moon system (bulge on the far side). Tide-generating force = gravitational attraction − centrifugal force.
[2:2:1] - Semi-diurnal tide: **two high and two low tides a day**; successive high tides **~12 h 25 min apart** (≈ **12 h 26 min**, NDA 2010).
[2:2:2] - The **lunar day is ~24 h 52 min**, so high tide comes **~52 min later each day**: **8:00 am → 8:52 am** (APPSC G1 2002, asked 3 times).
[2:2:3] TABLE head: Tide | Position | When
  [2:2:3.0] Spring (highest) | Sun, Earth, Moon in a straight line | New moon and full moon – twice a month
  [2:2:3.1] Neap (lowest range) | Sun and Moon at right angles | First and third quarter, ~7 days after spring tides – twice a month
[2:2:4] - Highest spring tides: full/new moon at an **equinox**; tides are larger when the Moon is at **perigee**. Ebb = falling tide, flow/flood = rising tide.
[2:2:5] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Geography - APPSC Exams 01 p.3-4, 03 p.9-11; app notes 4:2 sub 4
## SUB 3 | Day and Night, Day Length, Midnight Sun
[2:3:0] - **Day and night** are caused by the **rotation of the Earth on its axis** – not revolution, not tilt alone, not the Moon.
[2:3:1] - **Circle of illumination** = terminator = "**grey line**" (APPSC 2019); passes through both poles **only at the equinoxes**.
[2:3:2] - **Unequal day and night:** tilt + revolution. The difference is **least near the Equator** (~12 h day all year) and **increases away from it** to the poles (CDS 2014).
[2:3:3] - **Poles:** ~6 months of day + 6 months of night (**revolution with axial tilt**). **Midnight Sun** in the **Arctic and Antarctic regions** (beyond the polar circles) near the summer solstice; **Norway** = "Land of the Midnight Sun".
[2:3:4] - **Twilight** is shortest at the Equator (Sun rises almost vertically) and longest at high latitudes.
[2:3:5] - Equatorial seasons are marked by **wet and dry periods**, not by day length.
[2:3:6] ~~ Sources: NextGen Rocket General Geography #2 p.11-12, p.15; app notes 4:2 sub 2
## SUB 4 | Revolution, Seasons and Leap Year
[2:4:0] - Orbit slightly **elliptical**, Sun at one focus; period **~365¼ days** (365.25; sidereal year 365 d 6 h 9 min 9.5 s); mean speed **29.78 km/s**; the **Sun's gravity** is the centripetal force.
[2:4:1] - **Perihelion** early January (~147 million km; **3 Jan in 2026**), **aphelion** early July (~152 million km; **6 July in 2026**) – Earth moves faster at perihelion; the Earth–Sun distance is **not constant**.
[2:4:2] - **Seasons = revolution + 23½° tilt + parallelism of the axis**; Sun overhead at the Equator on **21 March and 23 September**, at Cancer on 21 June, at Capricorn on 22 December.
[2:4:3] - **Circular orbit instead of elliptical** → the varying distance would vanish, so the **difference between seasons would be reduced** (CDS 2011 key); the effect is small because tilt is the main cause.
[2:4:4] - Extra ¼ day a year → **leap year** of 366 days (29 February); a century year only if divisible by **400** (**2000 yes, 1900 no**).
[2:4:5] - Rotation gives day and night, time zones and Coriolis; **revolution** gives the year, seasons, solstices, equinoxes and perihelion/aphelion.
[2:4:6] ~~ Sources: NextGen Rocket General Geography #2 p.13-17, #3 p.19; app notes 4:2 sub 5

  x: Rotation is Earth's spin on its axis; revolution is its elliptical path around the Sun.

  x: The centrifugal effect of rotation is greatest at the Equator, so effective gravity and weight are least there.

  x: In the Northern Hemisphere the Coriolis deflection is to the right, not the left; deflection is to the left only in the Southern Hemisphere.

  *Kakinada | Nellore | Chennai | Puri
  x: Steering winds, trade winds and the Coriolis effect pushed Cyclone Montha north-northwest to the Kakinada coast of Andhra Pradesh.
[ne32b0c7fd54] sub2
  As the Earth rotates, what does a coastal place passing through two tidal bulges in a day generally experience?
  One high tide and one low tide | Four high tides and no low tide | Two high tides and one low tide | *Two high tides and two low tides
  x: Passing through two tidal bulges gives two high tides and two low tides, with similar tides about 12 hours 25 minutes apart.
[n5e29daa19e4] sub2
  What is the position of the Sun and the Moon when neap tides occur?
  In conjunction at new Moon | In line at full Moon | Both at their farthest from Earth | *At approximately right angles to each other
  x: Neap tides occur when the Sun and Moon are at about right angles; spring tides occur at new and full Moon when they are aligned.
[f7b14d93e42f4] sub2
  Successive high tides at a place occur about how far apart?
  *12 h 25 min | 12 h | 24 h 52 min | 6 h
  x: There are two high tides a day, about 12 h 25 min apart.
[fd28bc89327e9] sub2
  Consider the following statements about tides:
1. Spring tides occur at new moon and full moon.
2. Neap tides occur when the Sun, the Earth and the Moon are in a straight line.
Which of the statements given above is/are correct?
  *1 only | 2 only | Both 1 and 2 | Neither 1 nor 2
  x: Spring tides come when the Sun, Earth and Moon are aligned (new and full moon); neap tides come when the Sun and Moon are at right angles.
[gfc227d8300f0] sub2
  If high tide occurs at a port at 6:00 a.m. today, at about what time will it occur tomorrow?
  6:00 a.m. | 6:26 a.m. | *6:52 a.m. | 7:15 a.m.
  x: High tide comes about 52 minutes later each day, so 6:00 a.m. becomes about 6:52 a.m.
[g120423ed2500] sub2
  Why does high tide at a place come about 52 minutes later each day?
  *Because the lunar day is about 24 hours 52 minutes long | Because the solar day is longer than the sidereal day | Because the Earth's orbit is elliptical | Because the Sun's pull is stronger than the Moon's
  x: The Moon moves on in its orbit, so the lunar day is about 24 h 52 min and the tide comes about 52 minutes later.
[g1960d2c94cca] sub2
  How many times in a month do spring tides occur?
  Once | *Twice | Four times | Every day
  x: Spring tides come twice a month, at new moon and full moon, when the Sun, Earth and Moon are in line.
[ndbd7750db30] sub3
  Where are approximately 12 hours of daylight experienced throughout the year?
  Tropic of Cancer | Tropic of Capricorn | Arctic Circle | *Equator
  x: The Equator has about 12-hour days all year; variation in day length increases with latitude.
[n28417f6b920] sub3
  When does the circle of illumination pass through both poles?
  Perihelion | Solstices | *Equinoxes | Aphelion
  x: The circle of illumination (grey line) divides day from night and passes through both poles only at the equinoxes.
[n3843dba38ae] sub3
  What is the phenomenon called in which the Sun remains above the horizon for 24 hours during summer beyond the Arctic or Antarctic Circle?
  Twilight arc | *Midnight sun | Solar eclipse | Polar night
  x: The midnight sun refers to continuous daylight for 24 hours or more in high-latitude regions during their summer.
[n1401323b8f4] sub3
  How does the duration of twilight generally vary with latitude?
  It occurs only between the Tropics. | It is approximately the same at every latitude. | It is longest at the Equator and shortest at the poles. | *It is shortest at the Equator and becomes longer towards the poles.
  x: Twilight is shortest at the Equator because the Sun rises and sets nearly vertically. It lasts longer at high latitudes because the Sun follows an oblique path, becoming weeks-long near the poles.
[ngeneral-geography-3-15] sub3
  By what are seasons at the Equator mainly marked?
  *Wet and dry periods | Four distinct seasons | Six-month day and night | Winter and summer
  x: Day length hardly changes at the Equator, so it has wet and dry seasons rather than winter and summer.
[ngeneral-geography-3-39] sub3
  Where does the midnight sun occur?
  Between the two Tropics | Along the Tropic of Cancer | *North of the Arctic Circle and south of the Antarctic Circle | Between the Tropic of Cancer and the Arctic Circle
  x: The midnight sun occurs beyond the polar circles, peaking near each summer solstice, as in Norway.
[f7de514319e53] sub3
  By what other name is the circle of illumination known?
  *Grey line | Date line | Ecliptic | Great circle route
  x: The circle of illumination, or terminator, is also called the grey line or twilight zone.
[f643106e61e11] sub3
  Which country is known as the 'Land of the Midnight Sun'?
  Finland | Iceland | Canada | *Norway
  x: Norway, beyond the Arctic Circle, is called the Land of the Midnight Sun.
[fa0707433ff7c] sub3
  Why does twilight last longest at high latitudes?
  Rises and sets almost vertically | *Rises and sets along an oblique path | Is overhead at noon | Is closest to the Earth
  x: At high latitudes the Sun's oblique path makes twilight long, even weeks near the poles; at the Equator it rises almost vertically.
[g987951bb2f00] sub3
  Consider the following statements:
1. Day and night are caused by the rotation of the Earth.
2. Unequal lengths of day and night are caused by the tilt of the Earth's axis together with its revolution.
Which of the statements given above is/are correct?
  1 only | 2 only | *Both 1 and 2 | Neither 1 nor 2
  x: Rotation gives day and night; the tilted axis and revolution make day length vary through the year.
[g9142de2d824c] sub3
  If the Earth's axis were not tilted, which of the following would happen?
  *Day and night would be equal everywhere throughout the year | There would be no day and night | Days would be longest at the Equator | The poles would have six months of day
  x: Unequal day lengths come from the tilt; without it every place would have 12-hour days and nights all year.
[ngeneral-geography-3-17] sub3
  What causes the six-month polar day and night?
  Rotation alone | *Revolution combined with axial tilt | The Earth's elliptical orbit alone | The Moon's gravitational pull
  x: Revolution with axial tilt causes seasons, solstices and equinoxes, opposite seasons in the hemispheres and six-month polar day and night.
[ngeneral-geography-2-16] sub4
  Which of the following is NOT primarily associated with the Earth's rotation?
  *Perihelion and aphelion | Coriolis effect | Time zones | Day and night
  x: Perihelion and aphelion belong to revolution; day and night, the Coriolis effect and time zones come from rotation.
[ngeneral-geography-2-25] sub4
  The Earth takes about how many days to complete one revolution around the Sun?
  364¼ | *365¼ | 365 | 366
  x: Earth takes about 365¼ days to complete one revolution on its elliptical orbit.
[n5c90f6964c7] sub4
  What provides the principal centripetal force that keeps the Earth in its orbit around the Sun?
  The pressure of solar radiation | *The gravitational force of the Sun | The magnetic force of the Sun | The gravitational force of the Moon
  x: The Sun's gravitational attraction acts as the centripetal force that keeps the Earth revolving around it.
[na430c1cfbad] sub4
  Under the Gregorian rule, which of the following century years was a leap year?
  1700 | 1800 | 1900 | *2000
  x: A century year is a leap year only if divisible by 400, so 2000 was a leap year and 1900 was not.
[ngeneral-geography-2-38] sub4
  About how much is the extra fraction of a day in each calendar year that adds up to a leap day in four years?
  One-tenth of a day | One-half of a day | One-third of a day | *One-quarter of a day
  x: The extra quarter day accumulates to one day in four years, giving a 366-day leap year with 29 February.
[ff18e46769615] sub4
  What is the approximate mean orbital speed of the Earth around the Sun?
  1,670 km/s | 11.2 km/s | 7.9 km/s | *29.78 km/s
  x: The Earth revolves round the Sun at a mean speed of about 29.78 km/s.
[fccf711f04f69] sub4
  On which date did the Earth reach aphelion in 2026?
  3 January | 21 June | *6 July | 23 September
  x: In 2026 perihelion fell on 3 January and aphelion on 6 July.
[fd68216870ea2] sub4
  On which dates is the Sun overhead at the Equator?
  *21 March and 23 September | 21 June and 22 December | 3 January and 4 July | 1 January and 1 July
  x: At the equinoxes, 21 March and 23 September, the Sun is overhead at the Equator.
[fc0cb2dc52ca7] sub4
  By which number must a century year be divisible to be a leap year?
  4 | 100 | 40 | *400
  x: A century year is a leap year only if divisible by 400, so 2000 was a leap year and 1900 was not.
[fa0918e38f5b0] sub4
  Which of the following is NOT correct about the Earth's revolution?
  It takes about 365.25 days | Its mean speed is about 29.78 km/s | The orbit is slightly elliptical | *The Earth–Sun distance stays constant
  x: The orbit is slightly elliptical, so the Earth–Sun distance is not constant.

=== NOTES 4:3 | Concept of Time – Local and Standard Time, Gmt/utc, Indian Standard Time, Time Zones, International Date Line
## SUB 0 | Longitude and Local Time – Calculations
[3:0:0] # The rule (asked most: 23 PYQs)
[3:0:1] - Earth turns **360° in 24 h**, **west to east** → **15° = 1 hour**, **1° = 4 minutes**, **1' of arc = 4 seconds**.
[3:0:2] TABLE head: Longitude gap | Time gap
  [3:0:2.0] 1° | 4 minutes
  [3:0:2.1] 15° | 1 hour (60 min)
  [3:0:2.2] 90° | 6 hours
  [3:0:2.3] 180° | 12 hours (720 min)
  [3:0:2.4] 1' of arc | 4 seconds
[3:0:3] - **East is ahead (add), west is behind (subtract)**; moving westward local time **falls behind**. A place 30° west of Greenwich is **2 hours behind** GMT; the 15°E meridian is **1 hour fast**.
[3:0:4] - **Same side** of Greenwich → subtract the longitudes; **opposite sides** → add them (25°E and 35°W = 60° = **4 h**; 6°W and 9°E = 15° = 1 h).
[3:0:5] - **Same meridian = same local time**, however far apart north–south (NDA 2008: four places 500 km apart on one meridian all show **12:00 noon**).
[3:0:6] - Journey/message: arrival local time = departure + travel time ± longitude time. Flight 10:00 a.m., 3 h, 15° east → **2:00 p.m.**; telegram from Greenwich at noon, 12 min in transit, arrives 6:00 p.m. → 5 h 48 min = 348 min ÷ 4 = **87°E**.
[3:0:7] # Worked PYQs
[3:0:8] TABLE head: Given | Answer
  [3:0:8.0] A 6°W at 10 a.m. → B 9°E (APPSC G1 2023) | 15° = 1 h → 11 a.m.
  [3:0:8.1] A 60°W at 10 a.m. → B 90°E (APPSC G1 2019) | 150° = 10 h → 8 p.m.
  [3:0:8.2] 3:40 p.m. GMT → 10°W, West Africa (APPSC FSO) | 40 min behind → 3:00 p.m.
  [3:0:8.3] Noon GMT, place at 6 a.m. (UPSC 1998) | 90°W
  [3:0:8.4] Noon at IST meridian, place at 6 a.m. (IAS 1998) | 82°30'E − 90° = 7°30'W
  [3:0:8.5] Noon at 82°30'E, place at 6:30 a.m. (IAS 1994) | 5½ h = 82°30' west → 0°
  [3:0:8.6] Midnight IST, place at 6 a.m. (UPPCS 2002) | 82°30' + 90° = 172°30'E
  [3:0:8.7] Noon IST → 120°E (UPPCS 2001) | 37°30' × 4 = 150 min → 14:30
  [3:0:8.8] 10:00 a.m. IST → Shillong 92°E (IAS 1999) | 9.5° × 4 = 38 min → 10:38 a.m.
  [3:0:8.9] Greenwich noon → 5 p.m. local (UPPCS 2008) | 75°E
  [3:0:8.10] GMT 3 a.m., local 6 a.m. | 45°E
  [3:0:8.11] Cairo 2 h ahead of Greenwich | 30°E
  [3:0:8.12] Thimphu 90°E at Greenwich noon | 6:00 p.m.
  [3:0:8.13] Sunrise Tirap (Arunachal) 5 a.m. → Kandla (Gujarat, ~70°E) | ~25° ≈ 1 h 40 min → about 7:00 a.m. (nearest option)
[3:0:9] - **Ship** sailing west at the speed of rotation, 10:00 Monday at 90°W: it keeps pace with the Sun, so at the Date Line it is still **10:00 Monday** (UPPCS 2000).
[3:0:10] - **Rotation reversed** (UPSC 1997, asked twice): normally IST is 5½ h ahead of GMT and the 180°W side 12 h behind; reversed, IST would be 17½ h behind the Date Line → noon there = **18:30 hrs IST**.
[3:0:11] > Calculation angle: find the gap, × 4 min, then add (east) or subtract (west). Check the side of Greenwich first.
[3:0:12] ~~ Sources: PMF Physical Geography 03 p.47-51; NextGen Rocket Indian Geography #47 p.118; NextGen Rocket General Geography #2 p.17; app notes 4:3 sub 0
## SUB 1 | Indian Standard Time and the Standard Meridian
[3:1:0] # IST (asked 21 times)
[3:1:1] TABLE head: Item | Fact
  [3:1:1.0] Standard meridian | 82°30'E (82.5°E) – India's central meridian
  [3:1:1.1] IST vs GMT | GMT + 5 h 30 min (82.5 × 4 = 330 min), i.e. 5½ h ahead; 0000 UTC = 5:30 a.m. IST
  [3:1:1.2] Reference town | Mirzapur, UP (clock tower); the meridian passes just east of Prayagraj (Allahabad – Naini) – the town given in the keys
  [3:1:1.3] City closest (MPPSC 2008) | Rewa (MP, ~81°18'E)
  [3:1:1.4] States crossed, W→E | Uttar Pradesh, Madhya Pradesh, Chhattisgarh, Odisha, Andhra Pradesh – 5; not West Bengal (APPSC); last state = Andhra Pradesh (Kakinada district)
  [3:1:1.5] Meets the Tropic of Cancer | Koriya district, Chhattisgarh, on the Baghelkhand Plateau
  [3:1:1.6] Keeper | CSIR-National Physical Laboratory, New Delhi
[3:1:2] - **History:** India's time zones were set in **1884** – **Bombay Time** (GMT + 4 h 51 min) and **Calcutta Time** (GMT + 5 h 53 min 20 s); a single IST was adopted after Independence (**1947**; Calcutta Time lasted till 1948, Bombay Time till 1955). Calling Calcutta Time "4 h 51 min" and Bombay "5 h 30 min" is the false statement (APPSC flashcard).
[3:1:3] - **Basis of standard time:** a country's standard time is the local time of a **chosen longitude** (usually its central meridian), counted from Greenwich.
[3:1:4] - **City longitudes:** Delhi ~77°12'E – closest among the options is **Bengaluru** (~77°35'E); Hyderabad ~78°29'E, Nagpur ~79°05'E, Pune ~73°51'E (UPSC 2018).
[3:1:5] # India's span and the two-time-zone debate
[3:1:6] - India: **68°7'E (Guhar Moti, Kutch)** to **97°25'E** (Arunachal; easternmost between 97°25' and 97°30'E) → ~29° ≈ 116 min → **about 2 hours** between Arunachal and Gujarat (not 2½) (NDA 2017; Jharkhand 2023).
[3:1:7] - Latitudes **8°4'N** (mainland, Kanyakumari) to **37°6'N** (Indira Col); both extents **about 30°**. Older texts quote **8°4'28"N to 37°17'53"N**.
[3:1:8] - North-East loses daylight hours; proposals: advance IST by **30 min**, or a second zone ~1 h ahead. Assam tea gardens kept an informal **"Bagan time" (Chaibagaan time)**, 1 h ahead of IST – not official.
[3:1:9] - Andhra Pradesh: **12°41'N – 19°07'N**, ~77°E – 84°40'E.
[3:1:10] # IST in 2026 (current linkage)
[3:1:11] - **Jul 2026:** **White Rabbit** IST distribution network at the Regional Reference Standards Laboratory, **Jakkur (Bengaluru)** – sub-nanosecond sync over fibre, independent of GPS; the protocol was developed at **CERN** (not ISRO); "One Nation, One Time" (NPL–ISRO). It provides synchronised high-precision IST time-stamps for banking, payments, railways, airports, power grids and telecom, and guards against GPS spoofing.
[3:1:12] - **27 Aug 2026:** Consumer Affairs Ministry notified the **Legal Metrology (Indian Standard Time) Rules, 2026** – IST the single legal reference; in force **180 days** after Gazette publication (not immediately).
[3:1:13] > Trap: IST line misses **West Bengal**; 8 states on the Tropic vs 5 on the IST line, **Chhattisgarh** is on both.
[3:1:14] ~~ Sources: NextGen Rocket Indian Geography #45-47 p.116-120; PMF Physical Geography 03 p.47-50; Wikipedia "Time in India" (Bombay/Calcutta time, 1884); app notes 4:3 sub 3-4
## SUB 2 | Tropic of Cancer and India
[3:2:0] # Tropic of Cancer (asked 18 times)
[3:2:1] - At **23°26'N** (written **23½°N / 23°30'N**); divides India into **almost two equal halves** (not the international boundary).
[3:2:2] - Crosses **8 states**, W→E: **Gujarat** (1st), **Rajasthan**, **Madhya Pradesh**, **Chhattisgarh**, **Jharkhand**, **West Bengal**, **Tripura**, **Mizoram** (last).
[3:2:3] - **Not** crossed: **Odisha** (asked 4 times), **Uttar Pradesh**, **Bihar**, Himachal, J&K, Assam, AP. Old keys write "Bihar (Jharkhand)" – Jharkhand was then part of Bihar.
[3:2:4] - Wholly **north**: UP, Bihar, Punjab, Haryana, Himachal, Uttarakhand, Sikkim, Assam, Meghalaya, Arunachal, Nagaland, **Manipur**. **Andhra Pradesh lies wholly south**.
[3:2:5] - The line joining India's **easternmost and westernmost points lies north** of the Tropic; the maximum east–west extent is **not** along the Tropic (Jharkhand 2023: I and IV).
[3:2:6] TABLE head: City | Latitude
  [3:2:6.0] Gandhinagar (nearest, UPPCS 1999) | 23°13'N
  [3:2:6.1] Jabalpur (nearest, UPPCS 2017) | 23°11'N
  [3:2:6.2] Ujjain | 23°9'N
  [3:2:6.3] Ahmedabad | 23°2'N
  [3:2:6.4] Agartala | 23°50'N
  [3:2:6.5] Kolkata (nearest of Delhi, Jodhpur, Nagpur – IAS 2003) | 22°34'N
[3:2:7] - Other cities near it: Bhopal and Ujjain (MP), **Ranchi (Jharkhand)**, Agartala (Tripura), Aizawl (Mizoram).
[3:2:8] - **Mahi** crosses it **twice**: rises in MP, flows through Rajasthan and Gujarat into the **Gulf of Khambhat**.
[3:2:9] - World: crosses **17 countries** (Mexico, Egypt, Saudi Arabia, UAE, Oman, India, Bangladesh, Myanmar, China …) – **not Iran**. **Equator** passes Kenya, Colombia, **Indonesia**.
[3:2:10] - Globe: **179 parallels** and **360 meridians** at 1° interval.
[3:2:11] > NOT-angle: "Tropic does not pass through" → Odisha / UP; "lies north of the Tropic" → Manipur.
[3:2:12] ~~ Sources: NextGen Rocket Indian Geography #45-46 p.116-117; app notes 4:3 sub 6
## SUB 3 | Time Zones, GMT and UTC
[3:3:0] # Time terms
[3:3:1] TABLE head: Term | Meaning
  [3:3:1.0] GMT | Mean solar time at the Royal Observatory, Greenwich (0°); Prime Meridian adopted at the International Meridian Conference, 1884
  [3:3:1.1] UTC | World time kept by caesium atomic clocks; kept in step with the slowing Earth by leap seconds
  [3:3:1.2] Leap second | Added (e.g. 31 Dec 2016, just before midnight) because the Earth's rotation is slowing
  [3:3:1.3] Daylight Saving Time | Clocks advanced 1 h in summer to use evening light; India does not use it
  [3:3:1.4] Natural units | Day (rotation), month (Moon's revolution), year (Earth's revolution)
[3:3:2] - **NavIC** (7 satellites: 3 GEO + 4 GSO) times its signals with **rubidium** atomic clocks.
[3:3:3] # Zones
[3:3:4] - **24 theoretical zones** of 15° each; more in practice (half- and quarter-hour offsets).
[3:3:5] TABLE head: Country / city | Fact
  [3:3:5.0] France (with overseas territories) | 12 zones – most
  [3:3:5.1] Russia | 11
  [3:3:5.2] UK 9, Australia 9, Denmark 5, New Zealand 5 (with territories) | More than four (UPSC 2025: only four of five)
  [3:3:5.3] Brazil | Exactly 4
  [3:3:5.4] China | One zone despite its width
  [3:3:5.5] Colombo (Sri Lanka) | Same clock as Mumbai, Kolkata, Chennai (UTC+5:30)
  [3:3:5.6] Nepal | UTC+5:45
  [3:3:5.7] Accra, Dublin, Lisbon, London | GMT; not Madrid (CET) or Addis Ababa (UTC+3)
[3:3:6] - Ahead → behind GMT (UPSC 1995, asked twice): **Japan (+9) > Iraq (+3) > Greece (+2) > Cuba (−5) > Costa Rica (−6)**.
[3:3:7] ~~ Sources: PMF Physical Geography 03 p.47-48; NextGen Rocket General Geography #2 p.11; Wikipedia "Leap second" (31 Dec 2016); app notes 4:3 sub 1-2
## SUB 4 | International Date Line
[3:4:0] - Imaginary **pole-to-pole** line roughly along **180°** in the **middle of the Pacific Ocean** (APPSC, State PSC); **12 hours** (720 min) from Greenwich because it is the 180° meridian (A and R true, R explains A).
[3:4:1] - Nearest strait: **Bering Strait** (UPSC 2008, asked twice) – between the **Chukchi** (Russia) and **Seward** (Alaska) peninsulas.
[3:4:2] - **Zigzags** where **land is in the way**: Chukotka, Aleutians, Kiribati, Fiji, Tonga, Samoa.
[3:4:3] TABLE head: Crossing | Date change
  [3:4:3.0] Eastward (Asia → America) | Subtract a day (gain a day)
  [3:4:3.1] Westward (America → Asia) | Add a day (lose a day)
[3:4:4] - **Big Diomede (Russia) and Little Diomede (USA)** are neighbours on opposite sides, a day apart.
[3:4:5] - **Samoa** moved to the Asian side in **2011**; **Kiribati's Line Islands** (UTC+14) are first to see a new day.
[3:4:6] - Old UPPCS 1992 key: a ship's least-time path is "along the meridian" – strictly the shortest route is a **great-circle** route (meridians are great circles).
[3:4:7] ~~ Sources: NextGen Rocket Indian Geography #47 p.119; PMF Physical Geography 03 p.50-52; app notes 4:3 sub 5

=== OWN MCQs of 4:3 (69)
[n9384d02647c] sub0
  How much time does one minute of arc of longitude correspond to?
  4 minutes of time | 1 minute of time | 15 seconds of time | *4 seconds of time
  x: 15° = 1 hour and 1° = 4 minutes, so one minute of arc equals 4 seconds of time.
[nbe5f052963b] sub0
  Compared with a given place, what is the local time of places to its east?
  Ahead only in the Northern Hemisphere | Behind | The same | *Ahead
  x: The Earth rotates from west to east, so places to the east see the Sun earlier and their local time is ahead.
[n4c120fed181] sub0
  The longitudes of two places are 25°E and 35°W. What is their local-time difference?
  40 minutes | *4 hours | 10 hours | 2 hours 40 minutes
  x: The longitudes lie on opposite sides of Greenwich, so they are added: 25° + 35° = 60°. Sixty degrees correspond to four hours.
[ngeneral-geography-2-12] sub0
  What happens to local time when moving westward?
  Remains unchanged | Advances | *Falls behind | Changes by one full day
  x: Local time advances eastward, so moving west it falls behind (15° = one hour).
[nbab7e2559ee] sub0
  A place is situated 30° west of Greenwich. What will be its local time in relation to Greenwich Mean Time?
  1 hour ahead | *2 hours behind | 2 hours ahead | 30 minutes behind
  x: Thirty degrees correspond to two hours, and a place west of Greenwich is behind Greenwich Mean Time.
[n4313a321a18] sub0
  A flight departs from a western location at 10:00 a.m. local time, travels for three hours and reaches a location 15° east of the point of departure. What will be its local arrival time?
  *2:00 p.m. | 1:00 p.m. | 3:00 p.m. | 12:00 noon
  x: The journey takes three hours and the eastward movement adds one hour because 15° equals one hour. The arrival time is therefore 2:00 p.m.
[nindian-geography-47-18] sub0
  Fifteen degrees of longitude correspond to how much time?
  Four minutes | 15 minutes | 30 minutes | *One hour
  x: 15° equals one hour and 1° equals 4 minutes; IST (82°30′E) is GMT + 5 h 30 min.
[n5a0f46a7bf1] sub1
  Which one of the following is the correct reference location associated with the Indian Standard Time meridian?
  *Mirzapur | Kakinada | New Delhi | Jakkur
  x: Mirzapur in Uttar Pradesh is the reference town associated with the Indian Standard Time meridian.
[n211a02526f7] sub1
  The Indian Standard Time meridian passes through which one of the following sequences of States from west to east?
  *Uttar Pradesh, Madhya Pradesh, Chhattisgarh, Odisha and Andhra Pradesh | Uttar Pradesh, Rajasthan, Chhattisgarh, Odisha and West Bengal | Madhya Pradesh, Uttar Pradesh, Jharkhand, Odisha and Andhra Pradesh | Uttar Pradesh, Madhya Pradesh, Odisha, West Bengal and Andhra Pradesh
  x: The meridian crosses Uttar Pradesh, Madhya Pradesh, Chhattisgarh, Odisha and Andhra Pradesh. West Bengal is not included in this sequence.
[ne958281fff1] sub1
  A weather balloon launched at 0000 UTC goes up at what Indian Standard Time?
  *5:30 a.m. | 6:30 a.m. | 4:30 a.m. | 5:30 p.m.
  x: IST (82°30′E) is 5 h 30 min ahead of GMT, so 0000 UTC is 5:30 a.m. IST.
[n83bde8d6483] sub1
  Who maintains Indian Standard Time?
  India Meteorological Department, New Delhi | *CSIR-National Physical Laboratory, New Delhi | Survey of India, Dehradun | ISRO, Bengaluru
  x: IST is maintained by the CSIR-National Physical Laboratory, New Delhi.
[na591a96dd5c] sub1
  Which of the following pairs is NOT correctly matched?
