=== NOTES 4:4 | Seasons – Inclination of the Axis, Solstices and Equinoxes, Distribution of Insolation
## SUB 0 | Solstices, Equinoxes and Day Length
[4:0:0] # The four positions (asked most: 29 PYQs)
[4:0:1] TABLE head: Date | Event (NH) | Sun overhead at | NH | SH
  [4:0:1.0] 21 March (20/21) | Vernal (spring) equinox | Equator | Spring; day = night | Autumn begins
  [4:0:1.1] 21 June (20/21) | Summer solstice | Tropic of Cancer | Longest day | Shortest day
  [4:0:1.2] 23 September (22/23) | Autumnal equinox | Equator | Autumn; day = night | Spring begins
  [4:0:1.3] 22 December (21/22) | Winter solstice | Tropic of Capricorn | Shortest day | Longest day
[4:0:2] - Matches asked: winter solstice **22 Dec**, summer solstice **21 June**, vernal equinox **21 March**, autumnal equinox **23 Sept** (APPSC G1 2018, CDS 2014, WBCS 2020: all correct).
[4:0:3] - Order in a year: March equinox → June solstice → September equinox → December solstice.
[4:0:4] - **Equinox** = "equal night": Sun **overhead at the Equator**, **day = night everywhere** on Earth (SSC 2020); never at the Arctic Circle (NDA 2008 wrong pair). The tilt itself does not change – statement "day length changes because the tilt changes" is false (NDA 2009: I and II only).
[4:0:5] - "Vernal equinox": **20/21 March** in the NH; in the **Southern Hemisphere** the spring (vernal) equinox is **22/23 September** – so the APPSC flashcard key is "**both a and b**".
[4:0:6] - **Longest NH day:** **second half of June** (UPSC 2022, asked twice); **SH longest day: 22 December** (second half of December); **SH shortest day: 21 June**; NH shortest **22 December**.
[4:0:7] - On the shortest day in India (22 Dec) it is the **longest day in Australia** (also New Zealand) – Southern Hemisphere.
[4:0:8] - **Uttarayana** = the Sun's apparent **northward** movement, **22/23 Dec → 21 June**; Dakshinayana the reverse.
[4:0:9] - **21 June** = **International Day of Yoga** (UN, since 2015; 2026 theme "**Yoga for Healthy Ageing**").
[4:0:10] # Day length by latitude
[4:0:11] TABLE head: Latitude | 21 June | Equinoxes | 22 Dec
  [4:0:11.0] Equator | 12 h | 12 h | 12 h
  [4:0:11.1] Tropic of Cancer | ~13½ h | 12 h | ~10½ h
  [4:0:11.2] Arctic Circle | 24 h day – the Sun does not set | 12 h | 24 h night
  [4:0:11.3] North Pole | Continuous day | Sunrise / sunset | Continuous night
[4:0:12] - **Equator: day = night all year** (12 h on every date, incl. the September equinox) – at the June solstice the 12-h day is at the Equator.
[4:0:13] - **North Pole lit from 21 March to 23 September** (about **six months** of day, then six of night); when the North Pole has continuous daylight the South Pole has continuous darkness.
[4:0:14] - 23 Sept → 21 Dec: NH days **become progressively shorter**.
[4:0:15] - **June day length grows northward**: **Srinagar > Delhi > Hyderabad > Chennai > Kanyakumari/Thiruvananthapuram** – Srinagar has the longest summer sunlight, Delhi the longest June day of Delhi/Bhopal/Hyderabad/Chennai, Thiruvananthapuram the shortest.
[4:0:16] - **Midnight sun** = Sun above the horizon at midnight, beyond the polar circles in summer.
[4:0:17] > Date-match angle: memorise 21 Mar – 21 Jun – 23 Sep – 22 Dec and what is overhead on each.
[4:0:18] ~~ Sources: NextGen Rocket General Geography #1 p.5-6, #3 p.14-22; Britannica "equinox" (SH vernal equinox 22/23 Sept); app notes 4:3 sub 8, 4:4 sub 2-3
## SUB 1 | Overhead Sun, Shadows and Insolation
[4:1:0] # Vertical rays and shadows
[4:1:1] - Noon Sun overhead only **between the Tropics**, **twice a year** (**zero-shadow days**); places north of 23½°N – **Delhi, Srinagar** – **never** get vertical rays; **Hyderabad, Mumbai, Kolkata** (south of the Tropic) do (APPSC G1 2002).
[4:1:2] - **Andhra Pradesh** (12°41'N–19°07'N) gets the overhead noon Sun **twice a year**.
[4:1:3] - **21 June:** Sun overhead at the **Tropic of Cancer** – no noon shadow there; in north India the noon **shadow is smallest on 21 June** (UPPCS 2006), longest in December. On 21 June the Sun **does not set at the Arctic Circle** (UPSC 2019).
[4:1:4] - At the **Equator** there is no noon shadow in **March and September**.
[4:1:5] - **Delhi is hotter at noon in summer** because in winter the Sun's rays fall **more slanting (oblique)** – not because the Earth is nearer the Sun (IAS 1993).
[4:1:6] TABLE head: Basis | Summer | Winter
  [4:1:6.0] Hemisphere | Tilted towards the Sun | Tilted away
  [4:1:6.1] Rays | More direct | More oblique – same energy spread over a larger area
  [4:1:6.2] Days | Longer | Shorter
  [4:1:6.3] Sun altitude / shadow | Higher / shorter | Lower / longer
[4:1:7] # Insolation and latitude zones
[4:1:8] - Factors: **angle of incidence**, **duration of day**, **transparency of the atmosphere**, land configuration – **not** the direction of ocean tides.
[4:1:9] - Insolation generally **falls from the tropics to the poles**; maximum over **cloudless subtropical deserts**, not the Equator.
[4:1:10] TABLE head: Zone | Seasonal feature
  [4:1:10.0] Low latitudes | Small temperature contrast; wet and dry seasons
  [4:1:10.1] Mid-latitudes | Four distinct seasons
  [4:1:10.2] High latitudes | Greatest seasonal differences – day length, insolation and annual temperature range all largest
[4:1:11] - **Seasonal variation increases with latitude** → "seasonal differences are maximum in **high latitudes**" (APPSC G1).
[4:1:12] - **Southern Hemisphere**: hottest summer month **January**; **Christmas in Australia falls in summer** (UKPSC 2010).
[4:1:13] # Related one-liners
[4:1:14] - **Albedo** = share of the Sun's radiation reflected by the Earth's surface; **weather changes occur in the troposphere**, not the stratosphere – the **stratosphere** holds the ozone layer (**Ozonosphere**).
[4:1:15] - India: hot season March–May, ~**50°C at Ganganagar (1935)**; **IMD's four seasons** – winter, pre-monsoon, **South-West monsoon**, **post-monsoon**; traditional calendar: **six ritus**.
[4:1:16] - AP: SW monsoon (June–September) brings about **two-thirds** of the rain; winter October–February; coldest place **Lambasingi** (~2°C).
[4:1:17] ~~ Sources: NextGen Rocket General Geography #3 p.20-24, p.53; PMF Physical Geography 03 p.46; app notes 4:4 sub 4-7
## SUB 2 | Why Seasons Occur – Tilt, Revolution, Perihelion
[4:2:0] - **Seasons = revolution of the Earth + inclination (tilt) of its axis** (Jharkhand 2013, NDA 2014); **day-length variation** from season to season = **revolution on a tilted axis** (UPSC 2013, APPSC 2018) – not rotation, not the elliptical orbit, not oceans/mountains/altitude.
[4:2:1] - Axis tilted **23½° (23.5°) from the perpendicular** = **66½° to the orbital plane**.
[4:2:2] - **Parallelism**: the axis keeps pointing the same way (towards the **Pole Star**) through the year – because of **angular momentum** – so hemispheres tilt alternately towards (summer) and away (winter) → **opposite seasons**.
[4:2:3] - Full set: **axial inclination + parallelism + revolution + spherical shape** → changing **angle of insolation** and **day length**.
[4:2:4] - Tilt gives seasons, unequal days, the tropics and polar circles – **not day and night** (that is rotation, with the **circle of illumination** dividing day from night; rotation also gives the **Coriolis** deflection, to the **right in the NH**).
[4:2:5] - **What-if:** tilt raised from 23.5° to **30°** → **more intense seasonal contrasts**; axis **perpendicular** to the orbit → no seasons, equal day and night all year, Sun always over the Equator – but the North Pole would **not** lie always in darkness (NDA 2011).
[4:2:6] TABLE head: Point | Date | Distance | NH season
  [4:2:6.0] Perihelion (nearest the Sun) | ~3/4 January | ~147 million km | Winter
  [4:2:6.1] Aphelion (farthest) | ~4 July | ~152 million km | Summer
[4:2:7] - Earth is **nearest the Sun in northern winter**, so distance does not cause seasons (NDA 2014 trap).
[4:2:8] - Long term: **precession** (axial wobble) – **Vega** was pole star ~14,000 years ago and will be again in ~12,000 years; **Milankovitch cycles** drive long-term climate change. Melting ice moving mass away from the axis **slows rotation**.
[4:2:9] > Cause angle: "revolution on an inclined axis" is the answer; rotation and distance are the traps.
[4:2:10] ~~ Sources: NextGen Rocket General Geography #1 p.5, #2 p.16-18, #3 p.19; NextGen Indian Geography - APPSC Exams 01 p.10; app notes 4:4 sub 0-1, 4:3 sub 7

=== NOTES 4:5 | Interior of the Earth – Crust, Mantle, Core; Discontinuities and Seismic Evidence
## SUB 0 | Layers of the Earth and Their Composition
[5:0:0] # Crust, mantle, core (asked 11 times)
[5:0:1] TABLE head: Layer | Depth | State | Density (g/cm³) | Made of
  [5:0:1.0] Crust – outermost layer | Oceanic ~5 km, continental ~30 km (~70 km under mountains) | Solid | 2.8–3.0 | Continental: granite (Si, Al); oceanic: basalt (Fe, Mg)
  [5:0:1.1] Mantle – largest layer | Moho → 2,900 km | Solid, flows plastically (convection) | ~4.6 | Silicate minerals of iron and magnesium (upper mantle mostly silicates)
  [5:0:1.2] Outer core | 2,900–5,150 km (~2,260 km thick) | Liquid iron–nickel | ~10–12 | Fe + Ni
  [5:0:1.3] Inner core | 5,150–6,371 km (radius ~1,220 km) | Solid – immense pressure; P-waves at 11.23 km/s | 13.3–13.6 | Mostly iron
[5:0:2] - **Mantle = 83% of volume, 68% of mass** → the **largest** layer; **core = 16% of volume, 32% of mass**.
[5:0:3] - **Core is mainly iron** (with nickel) – not aluminium, chromium or silicon (UPSC 2009, asked twice). Its **lighter elements: silicon, sulphur, oxygen** – the APPSC G2 answer is **silicon**; not aluminium, magnesium, calcium or cobalt.
[5:0:4] - **Mantle = "mixed metals and silicates"** (iron–magnesium silicates) (SSC 2013); Sial is only silica + aluminium, Nife only metal.
[5:0:5] - **Lithosphere = crust + uppermost solid mantle** (~100 km) (NDA 2019).
[5:0:6] - Continental crust: thicker, older, granitic, lighter; oceanic crust: thinner, younger, **basaltic**, denser.
[5:0:7] - **Compositional layering:** crust, mantle, core. **Mechanical:** lithosphere, asthenosphere, mesosphere, outer core, inner core.
[5:0:8] # Suess's chemical layers
[5:0:9] TABLE head: Suess term | Elements | Feature
  [5:0:9.0] SIAL | Silica + Aluminium | Granite; forms the continents; density ~2.9
  [5:0:9.1] SIMA | Silica + Magnesium | Basalt; source of magma and lava
  [5:0:9.2] NIFE | Nickel + Ferrum (iron) | Core; very dense (~11); explains Earth's magnetism (SSC 2018; State PSC "Nickel and ferrum")
[5:0:10] - **Eduard Suess** coined SIAL, SIMA and NIFE.
[5:0:11] - **Whole Earth by mass:** **iron 32.1%** > oxygen 30.1% > **silicon 15.1%** > magnesium 13.9% > sulphur 2.9% > nickel 1.8% (RRB 2005: after iron and oxygen comes **silicon**).
[5:0:12] > Trap: crust question → oxygen/aluminium; whole-Earth question → iron.
[5:0:13] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Geography 02 p.2-5 (pp.16-19); NextGen Rocket General Geography p.28-29, p.31; app notes 4:5 sub 2, 5
## SUB 1 | Seismic Waves, Shadow Zone and the Seismograph
[5:1:0] # Seismic waves (asked 7 times)
[5:1:1] TABLE head: Basis | P (primary) | S (secondary) | Surface (L)
  [5:1:1.0] Type | Longitudinal, push–pull, compressional; vibration parallel | Transverse, shear; vibration perpendicular | Along the surface (Love, Rayleigh)
  [5:1:1.1] Speed | Fastest, arrive first | Slower | Slowest
  [5:1:1.2] Medium | Solid, liquid, gas | Solids only | –
  [5:1:1.3] Damage | Lower | – | Highest
[5:1:2] - An earthquake generates **P, S and L (surface) waves** – "P.S.L" (CGPSC 2013). P and S are **body waves**.
[5:1:3] - **P-waves are sound (acoustic) waves:** compressional like sound, and they become audible sound when they reach the air – so of P, S and L, **P-waves** are the sound waves.
[5:1:4] - Wave speed changes with density → **reflection and refraction** at boundaries reveal the layers. The **S-wave** behaviour (vanishing in the outer core) gave the key evidence of a **liquid outer core** – the CDS 2012 answer "secondary waves".
[5:1:5] - **Shadow zone:** P-waves strongly refracted at the mantle–core boundary → no direct P **~105°–145°** (Rocket: 103°–142°); **S-waves absent everywhere beyond ~105°**. The belt **105°–145°** gets **neither P nor S** (APSLPRB 2023).
[5:1:6] - Each earthquake has its **own** shadow zone, set by its epicentre (CGPSC 2020).
[5:1:7] # Recording
[5:1:8] - **Seismograph** records seismic waves (FBO). NCERT Class 8: the seismograph is a **vibrating rod** or pendulum that starts vibrating with the tremors.
[5:1:9] ~~ Sources: NextGen Rocket General Geography p.29-30; NextGen Indian Geography 02 p.1 (p.15); PMF Physical Geography 01 (P-waves "take the form of sound waves" in air); NCERT Class 8 Science ch.12 (seismograph); app notes 4:5 sub 1
## SUB 2 | Earth's Magnetism, Heat and Pressure
[5:2:0] # Magnetic field (asked 4 times)
[5:2:1] - **Cause:** electric **currents in the liquid outer core** – convection of molten iron = **geodynamo** (UPPCS 2011) – not a giant magnet at the centre. It shields life from **solar wind and cosmic radiation**; flow changes are linked to **reversals**.
[5:2:2] - **Magnetic axis ~11.5°** to the geographic axis (not 23½°).
[5:2:3] - **Magnetic equator:** field lines **horizontal**, dip = 0 (NDA 2017); it passes near **Thumba** (Kerala) – hence TERLS. At the magnetic poles the field is vertical.
[5:2:4] - **North magnetic pole:** first located (1831) on the **Boothia Peninsula, northern Canada** – UPSC 2005 key; it has since drifted into the Arctic Ocean towards Siberia (~86°N as of 2025, World Magnetic Model 2025).
[5:2:5] - **Direction of the field** at a place = **declination** (angle from true north) **and inclination/dip** (angle from horizontal) (CDS 2018).
[5:2:6] - ESA **Swarm** mission: outer-core flow under the Pacific switched from westward to eastward.
[5:2:7] # Heat, pressure, density
[5:2:8] - Temperature, pressure and density **all rise with depth**; ~**1°C per 32 m** in the upper crust. Earth's mean density **5.5 g/cm³** (densest planet).
[5:2:9] - Geothermal sites: **Puga Valley (Ladakh)**, Manikaran (Himachal), Tattapani (Chhattisgarh), Cambay (Gujarat), **Bakreshwar (West Bengal)**.
[5:2:10] - Diamonds rise from the mantle via **kimberlite pipes**; **Madhya Pradesh** ~90% of India's reserves.
[5:2:11] ~~ Sources: NextGen Rocket General Geography p.31; NextGen Indian Geography 02 p.4 (p.18); NOAA/BGS World Magnetic Model 2025 (north magnetic pole position); app notes 4:5 sub 6
[5:2:12] - **Isogonic lines** join points of **equal magnetic declination** (agonic line = zero declination); isoclinic lines join points of equal dip.
## SUB 3 | Discontinuities, Lithosphere and Asthenosphere
[5:3:0] TABLE head: Discontinuity | Separates | Note
  [5:3:0.0] Conrad | Upper and lower crust (SIAL–SIMA) | 1st from the surface
  [5:3:0.1] Mohorovicic (Moho) | Crust and mantle (APPSC G1) | P 6.9 → 8.1 km/s; 1909, Andrija Mohorovicic (Croatian)
  [5:3:0.2] Repetti | Upper and lower mantle | ~700–1,000 km
  [5:3:0.3] Gutenberg (Weichert–Gutenberg) | Mantle and outer core | ~2,900 km; S-waves vanish
  [5:3:0.4] Lehmann | Outer and inner core | ~5,150 km
[5:3:1] - 410 km and 660 km: mantle mineral **phase changes**. IUGG mantle zones: Moho–200, 200–700, 700–2,900 km.
[5:3:2] TABLE head: Basis | Lithosphere | Asthenosphere
  [5:3:2.0] Nature | Rigid; crust + uppermost mantle (~100 km) | Weak, plastic, partly molten (~100–200 km), in the upper mantle
  [5:3:2.1] Role | Broken into plates | Plates glide over it; main source of magma
[5:3:3] - Sequence: lithosphere → asthenosphere → mesosphere → **barysphere** (core).
[5:3:4] - Indian plate under Tibet: dense lower layer **delaminating** into the mantle at ~**100 km** (94 seismic stations).
[5:3:5] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Geography 02 p.2-3 (pp.16-17); NextGen Rocket General Geography p.29-30; app notes 4:5 sub 3-4
## SUB 4 | Sources of Information about the Interior
[5:4:0] TABLE head: Direct | Indirect
  [5:4:0.0] Surface rocks from mining areas and deep drilling (APPSC G1) | Seismic waves – most important
  [5:4:0.1] Volcanic material (magma, lava) | Gravity anomalies, magnetic surveys
  [5:4:0.2] Kola Peninsula (USSR, 1970s): ~12 km, deepest hole | Meteorites
  [5:4:0.3] Mponeng, TauTona (South Africa): ~3.9 km, deepest mines | Rise of temperature and pressure with depth
[5:4:1] > Trap: seismic, gravity and magnetic data are **indirect**; meteorites are indirect evidence.
[5:4:2] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Geography 02 p.5-6 (pp.19-20); app notes 4:5 sub 0

  Which layer lies directly below the mantle?
  *Outer core | Inner core | Crust | Lithosphere
  x: The crust is outermost, then the mantle, then the outer core, with the inner core at the centre.

  x: The mantle constitutes approximately 83% of Earth's volume and 68% of its mass.
[nacd0cec50a2] sub0

  x: The inner core is solid; only the outer core is liquid.
[n5cad1e9c6f2] sub0

  x: Density increases downward from approximately 2.8–3.0 g/cm³ in the crust to 4.6 in the mantle and 13.3–13.6 in the inner core.
[ngeneral-geography-4-1] sub0
  Which layer of the Earth is predominantly solid but behaves plastically over geological time?
  *Mantle | Crust | Outer core | Inner core
  x: The mantle is mostly solid but flows slowly over geological time, which drives convection. The outer core is liquid and the crust and inner core are rigid.

  x: The outer core contains liquid iron and nickel. The mantle lies below the crust, the inner core is solid iron and the core begins at about 2,900 km.

  x: By composition Earth has crust, mantle and core; mechanical layers are lithosphere, asthenosphere, mesosphere, outer core and inner core, so statement 2 is wrong.

  What is the nature of P-waves?
  Transverse (shake) | *Longitudinal (push–pull) | Rolling | Torsional
  x: In P-waves particles vibrate parallel to propagation (push–pull); in S-waves they vibrate perpendicular (transverse).

  x: S-waves are absent beyond 105°, so the S-wave shadow zone is larger than the P-wave shadow zone.
[ngeneral-geography-4-9] sub1
  Which of the following statements about seismic waves is incorrect?

  x: Option A is incorrect: S-waves travel only through solids and stop at liquids. P-waves arrive first, S-waves are shear waves and surface waves do most damage.
[ngeneral-geography-4-3] sub1
  S-waves disappear on reaching the outer core. In what state is the outer core?
  Solid | Plastic | Gaseous | *Liquid
  x: S-waves cannot pass through liquids, so they vanish at the liquid outer core.
[ngeneral-geography-4-11] sub1
  Between which angular distances does the P-wave shadow zone approximately lie?
  0° and 103° | 90° and 120° | 142° and 180° | *103° and 142°
  x: P-waves are strongly refracted at the mantle–core boundary, so they are not recorded about 105°–145° from the epicentre (often given as 103°–142°). Of the options, 103° and 142° is the only one that matches this zone.
[n62c86d66647] sub1
  Why do seismic waves help in identifying discontinuities inside the Earth?

  x: Changes in density alter seismic-wave velocity and produce reflection and refraction at boundaries, revealing internal discontinuities.

=== NOTES 4:6 | Rocks and Minerals – Igneous, Sedimentary, Metamorphic; the Rock Cycle
## SUB 0 | Igneous Rocks
[6:0:0] - **Formed by cooling and solidification of magma or lava** (State PSC); called **primary rocks**; **no layers, no fossils**; both **crystalline and non-crystalline** (State PSC: the true statement).
[6:0:1] TABLE head: Type | Formation | Examples
  [6:0:1.0] Intrusive (plutonic) | Cools slowly at depth → large grains / crystals | Granite (APPSC G2 2024), gabbro, diorite, pegmatite
  [6:0:1.1] Extrusive (volcanic) | Lava cools fast at the surface → fine grains | Basalt (APPSC FBO), rhyolite, andesite
[6:0:2] - **By silica:** rhyolite = felsic extrusive; granite = felsic intrusive; **diorite = intermediate intrusive**; basalt = mafic extrusive.
[6:0:3] - **Felsic:** silica-rich, light-coloured. **Mafic (basic):** rich in Fe, Mg, Ca; dark, dense.
[6:0:4] - **Not igneous:** **quartzite** (metamorphic, APSLPRB 2023), **dolomite** (sedimentary carbonate, NDA 2017). Granite and basalt = igneous (NDA 2007).
[6:0:5] - **Granite has no fossils** – formed from magma (JPSC 2013).
[6:0:6] # Deccan Trap basalt
[6:0:7] - The **Deccan Plateau / Deccan Traps** are made mainly of **basalt** – igneous lava flows (asked 4 times by APPSC).
[6:0:8] - Fissure eruptions at the end of the Cretaceous, **~65–66 million years ago** (APPSC Tech. Asst: nearest 65), as India passed over the Réunion hotspot.
[6:0:9] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Geography 02 p.6 (p.20); NextGen Rocket General Geography p.32; NextGen AP Geography 02 p.4 (Deccan Trap: Cretaceous fissure lava, basalt); app notes 4:6 sub 2, 9
[6:0:10] - **Olivine and pyroxene** are mafic minerals and **dark** in colour; felsic rocks are rich in quartz and feldspar, poor in Fe, Mg, Ca.
## SUB 1 | Composition of the Crust and Minerals
[6:1:0] # Elements of the crust (asked 8 times)
[6:1:1] TABLE head: Rank | Element | % by weight of crust
  [6:1:1.0] 1 | Oxygen | 46.6 – most abundant element (in oxides and silicates)
  [6:1:1.1] 2 | Silicon | 27.7 – second; not a metal; bulk of rocks and minerals as silica/silicates
  [6:1:1.2] 3 | Aluminium | 8.1 – most abundant metal
  [6:1:1.3] 4 | Iron | 5.0 – second-most abundant metal
  [6:1:1.4] 5–8 | Calcium, sodium, potassium, magnesium | 3.6, 2.8, 2.6, 2.1
[6:1:2] - Highest and lowest among O, Si, Ca, Na, Mg: **oxygen and magnesium** (CDS 2015).
[6:1:3] - "Element present in large amounts in rocks and minerals" = **silicon** (APPSC G1 2012).
[6:1:4] # Minerals (asked 7 times)
[6:1:5] - **Feldspar:** Si and O common to all feldspars; Na, K, Ca, Al in specific varieties; feldspar makes up about **one-half** of the crust (NCERT) – "one-fourth" is **wrong** (APPSC G1 2024 → **Statement I only**).
[6:1:6] - **Quartz** = SiO₂ (silicon dioxide); hard, resists weathering; Mohs 7; major part of granite and sandstone.
[6:1:7] TABLE head: Silicate class | Example
  [6:1:7.0] Inosilicate – single chain | Augite (pyroxene)
  [6:1:7.1] Inosilicate – double chain | Hornblende (amphibole)
  [6:1:7.2] Phyllosilicate (sheet) | Muscovite, biotite (mica) (APPSC FRO)
  [6:1:7.3] Tectosilicate (framework) | Albite, orthoclase (feldspar), quartz
[6:1:8] - **Bowen's reaction series** = order in which minerals crystallise from magma (APPSC FRO): **olivine first** → pyroxene → amphibole → biotite → K-feldspar → muscovite → **quartz last** (most stable).
[6:1:9] - **Mohs scale** measures **hardness of minerals** (APPSC G1 2002, repeated): talc 1, gypsum 2, calcite 3, fluorite 4, apatite 5, orthoclase 6, quartz 7, **topaz 8**, corundum 9, diamond 10. (Mercalli/Rossi-Forel = earthquake intensity; Beaufort = wind.)
[6:1:10] - **Calcium carbonate (CaCO₃):** limestone, **chalk and marble** (NDA 2020); **granite is not** a form of CaCO₃ (APPSC ASO 2017).
[6:1:11] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Geography 02 p.5 (p.19); NCERT Class 11 Fundamentals of Physical Geography ch.5 (feldspar one-half); PMF Physical Geography 01 (felsic, feldspar); app notes 4:6 sub 0, 6
## SUB 2 | Sedimentary Rocks, Fossils and Geological Time
[6:2:0] - Formed at the Earth's surface by the **hydrological system**, from **weathering of pre-existing rocks**; sediments are transported, deposited, compacted and **lithified** in **layers** (strata) → "stratified rocks"; they **contain fossils** – all four UPSC 2001 statements are correct.
[6:2:1] - Cover about **75% of the Earth's surface**; hold most fossils and **coal, petroleum, natural gas**.
[6:2:2] TABLE head: Mode | Examples
  [6:2:2.0] Mechanically (clastic) | Sandstone, conglomerate, shale, loess
  [6:2:2.1] Chemically | Rock salt, gypsum, halite, chert, limestone
  [6:2:2.2] Organically | Coal (Uttarakhand 2006), chalk, coral limestone
[6:2:3] - **Sandstone** forms **under water** (MPPSC 1993); the **Red Fort and Agra Fort** are built of red **sandstone** – sedimentary.
[6:2:4] - **Himalayan rocks** are mainly **sedimentary** (folded Tethys sediments) (AP surveyor 2017; Sachivalayam 2020).
[6:2:5] - **Limestone** occurs white as well as black; igneous rocks have no layers; fossils are **not** found in igneous rocks.
[6:2:6] # Porosity vs permeability (UPSC 2025)
[6:2:7] TABLE head: Term | Rocks
  [6:2:7.0] Porous and permeable | Chalk (very permeable, aquifers), sandstone
  [6:2:7.1] Porous but impermeable | Clay – very fine pores, poorly connected
  [6:2:7.2] Neither | Granite
[6:2:8] - UPSC 2025: Statement II (chalk porous) correct and explains I; Statement III ("clay not at all porous") **wrong** → "Only one of II and III is correct and that explains I".
[6:2:9] # Fossils and time
[6:2:10] - **Fossils** = preserved remains, impressions or traces of ancient organisms (SSC 2018).
[6:2:11] - **Dinosaurs: Mesozoic era** (Triassic, Jurassic, Cretaceous); died out ~66 million years ago.
[6:2:12] - **Age of the Earth:** **uranium (radiometric) dating**; carbon dating only for young organic remains.
[6:2:13] - Giovanni Arduino (1760) made the first geological time scale.
[6:2:14] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Geography 02 p.6 (p.20); PMF Physical Geography 02 (porosity); app notes 4:6 sub 3-4, 8
## SUB 3 | Metamorphic Rocks
[6:3:0] - Igneous **or** sedimentary rocks recrystallise under **heat and pressure** ("change of form") – they come from **both** (JPSC 2011).
[6:3:1] TABLE head: Parent rock | Metamorphic rock
  [6:3:1.0] Limestone (and chalk, dolomite) | Marble (UPPCS 1991; APPSC Panchayat Secretary)
  [6:3:1.1] Shale / clay | Slate (then schist)
  [6:3:1.2] Sandstone | Quartzite (UPSC 2001, asked twice) – not limestone, obsidian or shale
  [6:3:1.3] Granite | Gneiss
  [6:3:1.4] Basalt | Amphibolite, schist
  [6:3:1.5] Coal | Graphite (via anthracite)
[6:3:2] - Typical metamorphic minerals: **garnet** (UP 2010), kyanite, sillimanite, staurolite; **halite is not**.
[6:3:3] - Odd one out: **gneiss** (metamorphic) among sandstone, limestone, shale (sedimentary); **granite** (igneous) among marble, quartzite, slate (metamorphic); limestone among marble, slate, quartzite.
[6:3:4] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Geography 02 p.6 (p.20); app notes 4:6 sub 5
[6:3:5] - Match key "**coal → diamond**": both are carbon (diamond forms from carbon under extreme pressure); the true metamorphic series is coal → anthracite → **graphite**.
## SUB 4 | Petrology and the Rock Cycle
[6:4:0] - **Petrology** = science (study) of rocks, a branch of geology (APPSC G2 2019). Rocks: igneous, sedimentary, metamorphic.
[6:4:1] - **Rock cycle:** magma → (cooling) igneous → (weathering, erosion, deposition, **lithification**) sedimentary → (heat, pressure) metamorphic → (melting) magma; **any rock type can become another**; exposed metamorphic rock weathers into sediment.
[6:4:2] - Rainwater with dissolved **CO₂** forms **carbonic acid**, which weathers rocks.
[6:4:3] - **Enhanced rock weathering:** crushed **basalt** spread on farmland locks CO₂ as bicarbonate.
[6:4:4] - **Lithology** = physical character of a rock unit – texture, grain size, composition.
[6:4:5] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Geography 02 p.1 (p.15); app notes 4:6 sub 1, 7-8

[n1872c002539] sub0
  Which of the following is a coarse-grained intrusive igneous rock?
  Basalt | Rhyolite | *Gabbro | Obsidian
  x: Intrusive rocks cool slowly at depth, forming coarse grains, as in granite and gabbro.

  *Rhyolite | Diorite | Granite | Basalt
  x: Rhyolite is felsic extrusive; granite is felsic intrusive, diorite intermediate intrusive and basalt mafic extrusive.
[n4b3fe0fa94e] sub0
  Consider the following statements:
1. Felsic igneous rocks are generally light-coloured and silica-rich.
2. Mafic rocks are always less dense than felsic rocks.
Which of the above statement(s) is/are correct?
  *1 only | 2 only | Both 1 and 2 | Neither 1 nor 2
  x: Felsic rocks are silica-rich, light-coloured and relatively low in density; mafic rocks, rich in iron, magnesium and calcium, are dark and dense, so statement 2 is wrong.
[fc222e439d12a] sub0
  What are dark, dense igneous rocks rich in iron, magnesium and calcium called?
  Felsic | Intermediate | *Mafic | Siliceous
  x: Mafic (basic) rocks are rich in Fe, Mg and Ca; felsic rocks are silica-rich and light-coloured.
[fbdf338010a37] sub0

  Felsic extrusive rock | Mafic extrusive rock | Felsic intrusive rock | *Intermediate intrusive rock
  x: Diorite is intermediate intrusive; rhyolite is felsic extrusive, granite felsic intrusive and basalt mafic extrusive.
[ngeneral-geography-4-41] sub0
  Which of the following is NOT correct about rocks?
  Igneous rocks form by cooling of magma or lava | *Granite is extrusive igneous and basalt is intrusive igneous | Metamorphic rocks are existing rocks altered by heat or pressure | Sedimentary rocks form through surface deposition
  x: It is the reverse: granite is intrusive igneous and basalt is extrusive igneous.

  Iron | *Aluminium | Calcium | Silicon
  x: Aluminium (about 8.1%) is the most abundant metal in the crust, followed by iron (about 5%) and calcium (about 3.6%). Silicon (about 27.7%) is more abundant but is not a metal; oxygen is the most abundant element overall.

  x: Option C is incorrect: iron is about 5% and ranks fourth. Aluminium is third at 8.13%, after oxygen (46.60%) and silicon (27.72%).

[nfd23d6b10ad] sub2

  Slate | *Marble | Graphite | Quartzite
  x: Limestone becomes marble; shale becomes slate, sandstone quartzite and coal graphite.

  Lignite | Slate | *Graphite | Peat
  x: Coal is metamorphosed into graphite via anthracite; slate comes from shale.

  Gneiss is usually formed by the metamorphism of which rock?

  x: Gneiss usually forms from granite; limestone gives marble, sandstone quartzite and shale slate.

  Which metamorphic rock is formed from shale?

  x: Shale (clay rock) changes into slate under heat and pressure; slate can further become schist.

  In the rock cycle, through which process do deposited sediments become sedimentary rock?
  Melting | Metamorphism | *Lithification | Crystallisation
  x: After weathering, erosion and deposition, lithification forms sedimentary rocks; heat and pressure then form metamorphic rocks and melting forms magma.

  x: Exposed metamorphic rocks can weather into sediment; any rock type can be transformed into another.

  x: Lithology describes the physical character of a rock unit, particularly its texture, grain size and composition.

=== NOTES 4:7 | Earth Movements – Continental Drift, Sea-floor Spreading, Plate Tectonics and Plate Boundaries
## SUB 0 | Continental Drift – Pangaea and Gondwanaland
[7:0:0] # Wegener's theory (asked 11 times)
[7:0:1] - **Continental drift:** **Alfred Wegener** (German), **1912** – not Wilson, Du Toit or Hess.
[7:0:2] - Drift forces: **pole-fleeing force** (Earth's rotation) and **tidal force** (Sun and Moon) – **too weak**, so the theory was rejected.
[7:0:3] - Original single landmass: **Pangaea** (UPPCS 2016), surrounded by the mega-ocean **Panthalassa**.
[7:0:4] - Pangaea split into **Laurasia** (north) and **Gondwanaland** (south), separated by the **Tethys Sea**; the split began **about 200 million years ago** (NCERT; PMF); older books round Pangaea's break-up to **~225 million years** – the figure the app's MCQs use for Gondwanaland's break-up.
[7:0:5] TABLE head: Name | Present landmasses
  [7:0:5.0] Laurasia | North America, Greenland, Europe, Asia (minus India)
  [7:0:5.1] Gondwanaland | South America, Africa, India, Australia, Antarctica, Madagascar, Arabia
[7:0:6] - **India (Peninsular)** was part of **Gondwanaland** (IAS 1995, asked twice; APPSC) – not Laurasia; "all of the above" for South America, Africa, Australia (MPPSC 2008). **North America was not** part of Gondwanaland (UPPCS 2016).
[7:0:7] - APPSC G2 statements "Pangaea split 150 million years ago" / "Gondwanaland broke up 65 million years ago": NCERT puts the split at ~200 million years; dates differ between textbooks.
[7:0:8] # Evidence of drift
[7:0:9] TABLE head: Evidence | Example
  [7:0:9.0] Jig-saw fit | South America and Africa; Bullard's 1964 computer fit
  [7:0:9.1] Rock belts | ~2,000-million-year belt of the Brazil coast matches West Africa
  [7:0:9.2] Placer deposits | Ghana coast gold has no local source; gold-bearing veins in Brazil – the continents lay side by side (A and R both true, R explains A)
  [7:0:9.3] Tillite | Gondwana glacial sediments of India have counterparts in six southern landmasses
  [7:0:9.4] Fossils | Mesosaurus (South Africa and Brazil only); Glossopteris; lemurs
[7:0:10] - **UPSC 2025:** rock belts, Ghana gold from the Brazil plateau and the Gondwana counterparts – **all three** are evidence (NCERT wording).
[7:0:11] ~~ Sources: PMF Physical Geography 02 p.8-12; NCERT Class 11 Fundamentals of Physical Geography ch.4; app notes 4:7 sub 0-2
## SUB 1 | Plate Tectonics and the Plates
[7:1:0] # The theory (asked 6 times)
[7:1:1] - **Plate tectonics:** pieces of the Earth's crust (lithospheric plates) are in **constant slow motion driven by movement in the mantle** – rigid lithosphere on the semi-fluid **asthenosphere**.
[7:1:2] - Put together in **1967 by McKenzie and Parker**, and independently by **Morgan**; it is **based on sea-floor spreading**. It was **not given by H.H. Hess in 1967** (Hess gave sea-floor spreading, 1960–62) → CGPSC 2021: Statement I false, II true.
[7:1:3] - Order: 1912 Wegener → 1930s **Arthur Holmes** (convection currents; heat from **radioactive decay**) → 1960–62 **Hess** (sea-floor spreading) → 1963 Vine–Matthews → 1967 plate tectonics.
[7:1:4] # Plates
[7:1:5] - **Seven major plates** (APPSC 2020): **Pacific**, North American, South American, Eurasian, African, Indo-Australian, Antarctic.
[7:1:6] TABLE head: Minor plate | Location
  [7:1:6.0] Cocos | Between Central America and the Pacific plate
  [7:1:6.1] Nazca | Between South America and the Pacific plate
  [7:1:6.2] Caroline | Between the Philippine and Indian plates
  [7:1:6.3] Philippine | Between the Asiatic and Pacific plates
  [7:1:6.4] Arabian | Mostly the Saudi Arabian landmass
[7:1:7] - Cocos, Arabian, Philippine and **Nazca** are **minor** plates (NDA 2019).
[7:1:8] - Plate movement is monitored with **GPS** data.
[7:1:9] ~~ Sources: PMF Physical Geography 02 p.13-20; NextGen Rocket General Geography p.31-32; app notes 4:7 sub 0, 4
## SUB 2 | The Indian Plate and the Himalayas
[7:2:0] - **Palaeomagnetic** data show the Indian landmass has moved **northward** (UPPCS 1998) – from south of the equator; the Peninsula was joined to Africa until the **Cretaceous**.
[7:2:1] - **Deccan Traps** erupted as India crossed near the equator over the **Réunion hotspot**.
[7:2:2] - Collision with **Eurasia** began **~40–50 million years ago** (4–5 crore); the Tethys closed and its sediments were folded into the Himalayas.
[7:2:3] - India still moves north **~5 cm/year** → the **Himalayas are still rising** (~5 mm/year): A and R both true, R explains A.
[7:2:4] - Earthquakes from the **Himalayas to the Arakan Yoma** and in the **north-west** of the subcontinent: **collision of the Indian and Eurasian plates** – plate tectonic activity, not volcanoes, corals or dams.
[7:2:5] - The collision explains the Himalayas and the **Indo-Gangetic foreland basin**; **delamination** of the plate's lower layer at ~100 km under southern Tibet.
[7:2:6] ~~ Sources: PMF Physical Geography 02 p.34 (India's northward journey); NextGen Rocket Indian Geography (Himalaya origin); app notes 4:7 sub 6
## SUB 3 | Sea-floor Spreading and Palaeomagnetism
[7:3:0] - **Sea-floor spreading** (Harry Hess, 1960–62): magma rises at mid-ocean ridges → **new crust is continually added**; old crust is destroyed by **subduction** – the two roughly balance.
[7:3:1] - Away from the ridge, the **age, density and thickness** of oceanic crust and its sediments **increase** (APPSC).
[7:3:2] - Ocean-floor rocks are young; the oldest are ~**200 million years**.
[7:3:3] - Rocks record the field of their time (**palaeomagnetism**); **Vine and Matthews (1963)**: normal and reversed bands lie **symmetrically** on either side of the ridge.
[7:3:4] - **UPSC 2018:** the Earth's magnetic field **has reversed** every few hundred thousand years (A true); early atmosphere had **no 54% oxygen** (B false); living organisms **modified** the early atmosphere (C true) → **A and C only**.
[7:3:5] - Spreading comes in **bursts**: one observed episode – seafloor sank 4 m, spread over 1 m, 160 million m³ of lava in 16 days.
[7:3:6] ~~ Sources: PMF Physical Geography 02 p.13-16; NCERT Class 11 Fundamentals ch.2 (evolution of the atmosphere); app notes 4:7 sub 3
## SUB 4 | Plate Boundaries and Earth Movements
[7:4:0] TABLE head: Boundary | Motion | Examples
  [7:4:0.0] Divergent (constructive) | Apart; new crust | Mid-Atlantic Ridge; Red Sea, East African Rift (Nubian, Somalian, Arabian plates near the Afar triple junction)
  [7:4:0.1] Convergent (destructive) | Collision / subduction; mountains, trenches | Himalayas (continent–continent); Andes (Nazca under South American); Mariana Trench (ocean–ocean)
  [7:4:0.2] Transform (conservative) | Slide past | San Andreas (not divergent); Sagaing Fault (Myanmar, Indian–Eurasian)
[7:4:1] - No new crust at convergent boundaries; earthquakes there come from subduction friction (Ring of Fire).
[7:4:2] - **Owen Fracture Zone** separates the Arabian and Indian plates. **Ladakh Magmatic Arc:** northward subduction of the **Neo-Tethys** plate.
[7:4:3] - **Sudden movements** inside the Earth cause earthquakes and **volcanoes** (APPSC AEE); slow (diastrophic) movements build continents and mountains.
[7:4:4] ~~ Sources: PMF Physical Geography 02 p.17-22; NextGen Rocket General Geography p.32; app notes 4:7 sub 5, 7

=== NOTES 4:8 | Folding, Faulting, Mountain Building; Isostasy
## SUB 0 | Types of Mountains – Fold, Block, Volcanic, Residual, Dome
[8:0:0] # Five types (asked 15 times)
[8:0:1] TABLE head: Type | How formed | Features | Examples
  [8:0:1.0] Young fold | Tertiary (Alpine) compression of geosyncline sediments | Very high, rugged, sharp conical peaks; world's highest peaks | Himalaya, Alps, Andes, Rockies, Atlas
  [8:0:1.1] Old fold | Caledonian/Hercynian or Precambrian folding + long denudation | Low, rounded, highly eroded | Aravallis (Precambrian), Appalachians, Urals, Pennines
  [8:0:1.2] Block | Faulting – uplifted block (horst) beside a sunken block (graben) | Steep fault scarps; linked with rift valleys | Sierra Nevada, Vosges (France), Black Forest (Germany), Salt Range
  [8:0:1.3] Volcanic | Accumulation of lava and ash around a vent | Conical, with a crater | Kilimanjaro, Fujiyama, Etna (Sicily), Mount St. Helens, Cotopaxi
  [8:0:1.4] Residual | Erosion leaves the resistant core of an old range | Remnants of older surfaces | Aravallis, Nilgiris, Vindhyas, Appalachians, Pennines
  [8:0:1.5] Dome | Magma pushes the crust up without erupting | Dome-shaped uplift | Black Hills, Bighorns (USA)
[8:0:2] - **Mountain:** most geologists take a landform rising at least **300 m (1,000 ft)** above its surroundings as a mountain (APPSC FSO).
[8:0:3] - **World's highest peaks** (Everest) lie in **young fold** mountains; Everest and Mont Blanc are fold, **not volcanic**.
[8:0:4] - **Young vs old:** **Alps** is the only young fold range among Appalachians, Aravallis, Alps, Urals (APPSC G2); the **Andes** are young, not old (UKPSC 2016); the **Appalachians** are not Tertiary (UPPSC 2014 – Kunlun, Alps, Andes are). Pennines, Appalachians, Aravallis = **old mountains** (State PSC).
[8:0:5] - **Block:** **Sierra Nevada** is a block mountain, not young fold (MPPSC 2021); **Black Forest = Germany** (Feldberg 1,493 m), Vosges = France, across the Rhine graben.
[8:0:6] - **Residual mountain of India = Aravallis** (UPPSC 2005; APPSC Asst Director). Match (UP UDA 2013): **Alps – folded, Vosges – block, Vindhya – residual, Fujiyama – volcanic**.
[8:0:7] - **Volcanic mountain:** **Mount Etna** (APPSC G1); Kilimanjaro, Fuji.
[8:0:8] - **Highest of Cotopaxi, Kilimanjaro, Pinatubo, Taal:** **Cotopaxi** (~5,897 m, Ecuador) just above Kilimanjaro (5,895 m) (UPPSC 2015). Highest volcano overall: Ojos del Salado (6,893 m).
[8:0:9] - **Andes** (~7,000 km) = longest range; Himalayas = highest.
[8:0:10] - **"Island of precipitation":** **dome** mountains like the Black Hills rise abruptly from dry plains and get about twice the plains' rainfall (APPSC G2 2011, printed as "Down mountains").
[8:0:11] > Trap: Aravallis are both old fold and residual; when "old mountains" and "fold mountains" are both offered, the books' key is "old mountains".
[8:0:12] ~~ Sources: NextGen Rocket General Geography p.33-34; NextGen Indian Geography 02 p.7-8 (pp.21-22); PMF Physical Geography 02 p.46-48 (pp.133-135); NPS/NWS Black Hills climate; app notes 4:8 sub 1
[8:0:13] - **Appalachians** = old fold mountains of the **Caledonian** orogeny (Palaeozoic).
[8:0:14] - **Eastern Ghats:** old, highly eroded, **discontinuous** residual hills of the stable **Gondwana (Peninsular) shield** – not a plate-boundary or young fold range.
## SUB 1 | The Aravalli – India's Oldest Fold (Residual) Range
[8:1:0] - **Oldest mountain range of India** (asked 6 times: vs Himalaya, Shiwalik, Vindhya, Satpura, Nilgiri, Western Ghats); Precambrian, from Dharwar sediments; age about **570 million years** (IAS 2001).
[8:1:1] - **Old fold mountain** (APPSC FSO flashcard: "fold mountain") and a **residual** mountain – "continuously denuded, typical residual hills" 400–600 m.
[8:1:2] - Runs ~800 km from **Delhi** through **Haryana** and **Rajasthan** to northern **Gujarat** – mainly **Rajasthan** (UPRO 2014).
[8:1:3] TABLE head: Peak | Height | Note
  [8:1:3.0] Guru Shikhar | 1,722 m | Highest; Mount Abu, Sirohi district, Rajasthan (UPSC 2007, asked twice)
  [8:1:3.1] Kumbhalgarh | ~1,224 m | Highest when Guru Shikhar is not offered (RAS 2012)
  [8:1:3.2] Sajjangarh, Lilagarh, Taragarh | ~938, 874, 873 m | Lower
[8:1:4] - Guru Shikhar is also the **highest peak of the Central Highlands** of the Peninsular Plateau (APPSC Degree Lecturers 2020); Parasnath and Dalma are Chotanagpur, Kamet is Himalaya.
[8:1:5] - **Supreme Court (Nov 2025):** an Aravalli **hill** = landform rising at least **100 m** above local relief (not 500 m); an Aravalli **range** = hills lying within **500 m** of each other; new mining leases paused. MoEF&CC banned new leases on **24 Dec 2025**; the SC later stayed its own directions (Dec 2025).
[8:1:6] ~~ Sources: NextGen Rocket Indian Geography p.43; NextGen Indian Geography 05 p.10; PMF Physical Geography 02 p.48 (p.135); app notes 4:8 sub 2 (SC 2025 items, as of 2026)
## SUB 2 | Faults, Block Mountains and Rift Valleys
[8:2:0] # Faults
[8:2:1] TABLE head: Fault | Force | Movement
  [8:2:1.0] Normal | Tension | Hanging wall down
  [8:2:1.1] Reverse | Compression | Hanging wall up
  [8:2:1.2] Thrust | Compression | Low-angle reverse
  [8:2:1.3] Strike-slip | Shear | Horizontal
[8:2:2] - **Horst** = uplifted fault block → **block mountain** (APPSC AEE 2023); **graben** = down-faulted block → **rift valley**.
[8:2:3] - **Rift valley:** tension → faulting → central block subsides → elongated depression. Examples: **Rhine** (between Vosges and Black Forest), **Great Rift Valley** (East Africa), **Death Valley** (California – UPPSC 2012), **Narmada, Tapi**. Sierra Nevada = horst.
[8:2:4] # UPSC 2024 (asked twice): mountain – region – type
[8:2:5] TABLE head: Row | Correct?
  [8:2:5.0] Central Asia – Vosges – fold | Wrong – Vosges is a block mountain in Europe
  [8:2:5.1] Europe – Alps – block | Wrong – Alps are fold
  [8:2:5.2] North America – Appalachians – fold | Correct
  [8:2:5.3] South America – Andes – fold | Correct
[8:2:6] - Answer: **only two** rows correct.
[8:2:7] # Rift valleys of India (asked 6 times)
[8:2:8] - **Narmada** flows **west** through a **rift valley (fault trough)** between the **Vindhyas (north)** and **Satpuras (south)** → valley of **structural** origin (CDS 2010); forms an **estuary, no delta** (A false, R true – UPPSC 2005).
[8:2:9] - Why the Narmada flows west: **only because it occupies a rift valley** (UPSC 2015: "Only I"); lying between Vindhya and Satpura is just its location.
[8:2:10] - **Damodar** = east-flowing river in a **rift valley due to down-warping** (IAS 1998). Tapi also flows in a rift.
[8:2:11] ~~ Sources: NextGen Rocket General Geography p.36; NextGen Indian Geography 02 p.8 (p.22); NextGen Indian Geography 05 (Narmada rift); NCERT Class 11 India Physical Environment ch.3; app notes 4:8 sub 4
[8:2:12] - **Satpura** = **block mountain (horst)** standing between the rift valleys (grabens) of the **Narmada (north) and Tapi (south)**.
[8:2:13] - **East African Rift lakes:** Turkana, Albert, Edward, Kivu, **Tanganyika** and **Malawi (Nyasa) at the southern end**; **Lake Victoria is NOT a rift lake** – it fills a shallow basin between the two arms of the rift.
[8:2:14] - **Himalayan MCT, MBT and HFF** (Main Central Thrust, Main Boundary Thrust, Himalayan Frontal Fault) are **thrust faults**, younger southwards.
[8:2:15] - **Indo-Gangetic Plain** (a foreland basin) is bounded by the **Himalayan Frontal Fault** in the north and a **normal fault** along the edge of the Peninsula in the south.
## SUB 3 | Diastrophism – Endogenic and Exogenic Forces
[8:3:0] - **Endogenic** (internal heat → mantle dynamics → plate movement): **diastrophism and volcanism**; **earthquakes are endogenic** (APPSC Inspector of Factories). **Exogenic:** weathering, erosion, deposition.
[8:3:1] - **Diastrophism** = all processes that move, raise or build up the crust: orogeny, epeirogeny, **earthquakes**, **plate tectonics**; **"heterogenic" is NOT** one (MPPSC 2023).
[8:3:2] TABLE head: Orogenic | Epeirogenic
  [8:3:2.0] Mountain building; severe folding in narrow belts | Continent building; uplift or warping of large areas
  [8:3:2.1] Horizontal (tangential) forces → folding (State PSC) | Vertical (radial) forces (UKPSC 2024)
[8:3:3] - **APPSC G1 – incorrect statement:** "Orogeny is the continental building process" – orogeny is **mountain** building; epeirogeny is continental building.
[8:3:4] ~~ Sources: NCERT Class 11 Fundamentals of Physical Geography ch.5; NextGen Rocket General Geography p.31; app notes 4:8 sub 0
[8:3:5] - **Slow v. sudden:** orogeny and epeirogeny are the slow **diastrophic** movements; the **sudden** endogenic movements – **earthquakes and volcanism** – are treated separately (Savindra Singh). Uplift or warping of whole continents is **epeirogeny**, not orogeny.
[8:3:6] - **Epeirogenic example:** vertical movements of the **Peninsular Block** (marine transgression and regression).
## SUB 4 | Young Fold Mountains – Evidence from the Himalaya
[8:4:0] - **Youngest/most recent range:** the **Himalayas** (CGPSC 2018); within them the **Shiwaliks** are the youngest chain (UPPSC 2010).
[8:4:1] - **Evidence of youth** (UPSC 2012, asked twice → **all four**): **deep gorges**, **U-turn river courses**, **parallel mountain ranges**, **steep gradients causing landslides**.
[8:4:2] - Formed by the **northward movement of the Indian Plate** squeezing the **Tethys** sediments; main uplift Eocene–Pliocene.
[8:4:3] - **Tertiary (Alpine) orogeny:** Himalaya, Alps, Andes, Rockies, Atlas, Kunlun. **Caledonian:** Appalachians (old).
[8:4:4] ~~ Sources: NCERT Class 11 India Physical Environment ch.2; PMF Physical Geography 02 p.48 (p.135); NextGen Rocket General Geography p.33; app notes 4:8 sub 1
## SUB 5 | Folds
[8:5:0] - **Anticline** = **upfold** (arch; oldest strata in the core) (NDA 2015); **syncline** = downfold (trough).
[8:5:1] - Rising pressure: symmetrical → asymmetrical → overturned → **recumbent** (axial plane horizontal; **both limbs horizontal and parallel** – NDA 2019) → **nappe** (recumbent fold broken and thrust far forward).
[8:5:2] - **Isoclinal** fold = limbs parallel to each other (and to the axial plane).
[8:5:3] - Shiwaliks: simple anticline–syncline folds (Jura type). Cuddapah sediments lie in **synclinal troughs**.
[8:5:4] ~~ Sources: PMF Physical Geography 02 p.47-49 (pp.134-136); app notes 4:8 sub 3
## SUB 6 | Isostasy and Geosynclines
[8:6:0] - **Isostasy:** crustal blocks float in balance on denser material below.
[8:6:1] TABLE head: Airy | Pratt
  [8:6:1.0] Uniform density, varying thickness | Varying density, same depth of compensation
  [8:6:1.1] Mountains have deep roots | Higher blocks are lighter
[8:6:2] - **Kober's geosynclinal theory:** long shallow sediment-filled troughs (geosynclines) between rigid forelands (**kratogens**) are squeezed into mountains; Tethys → Himalaya. Stages: **lithogenesis** (sedimentation) → **orogenesis** → **gliptogenesis**.
[8:6:3] ~~ Sources: app notes 4:8 sub 5; PMF Physical Geography 02 p.47 (geosyncline)

[fe6844070af5c] sub2

  x: A normal fault forms under tension with the hanging wall moving down; a reverse fault forms under compression with it moving up.

  x: A horst is an uplifted block between faults, so it forms a block mountain; a sunken block (graben) forms a rift valley.

[gc1b5e1706357] sub2
  Which force produces a normal fault?
  *Tension | Compression | Shear | Rotation
  x: A normal fault forms under tension, with the hanging wall moving down; reverse and thrust faults form under compression.
[gb4357d978b5d] sub2
  In a reverse fault, which force acts and how does the hanging wall move?
  Tension – hanging wall moves down | Shear – horizontal movement | *Compression – hanging wall moves up | Tension – hanging wall moves up
  x: A reverse fault forms under compression and its hanging wall moves up; a normal fault forms under tension.
[ngeneral-geography-4-38] sub3
  What do mantle dynamics, produced by endogenic processes, in turn produce?
  Weathering | Erosion | *Plate movement | Deposition
  x: Endogenic processes produce mantle dynamics, and mantle dynamics produce plate movement; weathering and erosion are exogenic.
[n0ad90b26a84] sub3
  What are mountain-building movements caused mainly by horizontal (tangential) forces called?
  Epeirogenic movements | Isostatic movements | *Orogenic movements | Eustatic movements
  x: Orogenic movements build mountains through horizontal forces in narrow belts; epeirogenic movements are vertical uplift or subsidence of large areas.
[n728edd55105] sub3
  The vertical movements of the Peninsular Block, including transgression and regression, are examples of which type of diastrophic effect?
  Glacial effect | Volcanic effect | *Epeirogenic effect | Orogenic effect
  x: The Peninsular Block largely escaped folding but experienced vertical movements such as transgression and regression, representing an epeirogenic effect.
[n5d6d6f3fbab] sub3
  What are the processes that move, raise or build up the Earth's crust, including earthquakes and plate movements, collectively called?
  *Diastrophism | Denudation | Weathering | Mass wasting
  x: Diastrophism covers all crustal movements that move, raise or build up the crust, including earthquakes and plate movements, not just folding.
[ffa43c8040ea8] sub3
  Weathering and erosion are examples of which processes?
  Endogenic processes | Orogenic movements | Epeirogenic movements | *Exogenic processes
  x: Weathering and erosion are exogenic processes, not part of diastrophism.
[f3f57f7798bd9] sub3
  What is mountain building by folding and faulting due to horizontal (tangential) forces called?
  Epeirogenic movement | *Orogenic movement | Volcanism | Weathering
  x: Orogenic movements act through horizontal forces and build mountains such as the Himalayas.
[f767694ba01c5] sub3
  Which forces mainly cause epeirogenic movements?
  Horizontal forces | Shear forces | *Vertical forces | Tidal forces
  x: Epeirogenic movements are vertical and uplift or warp continents and plateaus.
[fb9be36dbe259] sub3
  Consider the following statements about diastrophism:
1. Orogenic movements uplift or warp whole continents and plateaus.
2. Sudden movements such as earthquakes and volcanoes are treated separately.
Which of the statements given above is/are correct?
  1 only | *2 only | Both 1 and 2 | Neither 1 nor 2
  x: Uplift or warping of continents is epeirogenic, not orogenic; sudden movements are treated separately.
[n95620fa7193] sub4
  As a result of which of the following were the Himalaya formed through the uplift of Tethys sediments?
  The vertical subsidence of the Eurasian Plate during the Palaeozoic | The southward movement of the Indian Plate during the Precambrian | The volcanic eruption of the Deccan Plateau during the Quaternary | *The northward movement of the Indian Plate from the Eocene to the Pliocene
  x: The Himalaya formed when Tethys sediments were pushed upward by the northward-moving Indian Plate. Uplift began about 70 million years ago and continues even today.
[na6e40bdf9b1] sub4
  Which of the following is NOT correct about the Himalayas?
  Their formation began about 70 million years ago and continues at present | They were formed from sediments of the Tethys Sea | Their uplift is associated with the Eocene, Miocene and Pliocene epochs | *The Siwaliks are the oldest Himalayan range
  x: The Siwaliks are the youngest Himalayan range, not the oldest.
[n0fc37e7328b] sub5
  What is a recumbent fold that is broken and thrust far forward called?
  Isoclinal fold | Overturned fold | Recumbent fold | *Nappe
  x: A nappe is a strongly displaced recumbent fold that has been broken and thrust far forward.
[n07239fe3205] sub5
  Which of the following pairs is correctly matched?
  Siwaliks – Block mountains | Siwaliks – Horst | Siwaliks – Rift valley | *Cuddapah sediments – Synclinal troughs
  x: Cuddapah sediments were laid down in synclinal troughs; the Siwaliks show simple Jura-type anticline–syncline folds, not block structures.
[fab29e2b20c88] sub5
  What is a fold whose two limbs are parallel called?
  Recumbent fold | *Isoclinal fold | Symmetrical fold | Anticline
  x: An isoclinal fold has parallel limbs.
[f4f75e7f169a9] sub5
  Which fold form develops immediately before a nappe?
  Symmetrical fold | Asymmetrical fold | Isoclinal fold | *Recumbent fold
  x: With rising pressure a recumbent fold breaks along a thrust and is pushed forward to form a nappe.
[n52b355c6c91] sub5
  Consider the following statements about the development of folds with increasing pressure:
1. A symmetrical fold comes before an asymmetrical fold.
2. A nappe forms before a recumbent fold.
3. A recumbent fold forms after an asymmetrical fold.
Which of the statements given above is/are correct?
  1 and 2 only | 2 and 3 only | *1 and 3 only | 1, 2 and 3
  x: The order is symmetrical, asymmetrical, recumbent and finally nappe, so a nappe forms after (not before) a recumbent fold.
[nfceb4d08ed4] sub5
  What are an up-arched fold and a down-troughed fold respectively called?
  Syncline and anticline | *Anticline and syncline | Horst and graben | Nappe and overfold
  x: An anticline is an up-arched fold, while a syncline is a down-troughed fold. Horst and graben are fault blocks, not folds.
[f1058312589cf] sub5
  With rising pressure, which fold forms next after an asymmetrical fold?
  Symmetrical fold | Nappe | *Overfold | Anticline
  x: The sequence is symmetrical, asymmetrical, overfold, recumbent and nappe.
[f620abdef0aa0] sub5
  Which is the youngest Himalayan range, with simple Jura-type folds?
  Greater Himalaya | *Siwaliks | Lesser Himalaya | Karakoram
  x: The Siwaliks are the youngest Himalayan range and show simple Jura-type folds.
[n87535bec26c] sub6
  What is isostasy?
  Continuous erosion of mountains by running water | Horizontal movement of plates across ocean floors | Formation of folds exclusively by compression | *Balance of crustal blocks floating on denser material below
  x: Isostasy refers to the gravitational balance of crustal blocks floating on denser underlying material.
[n5d5651bac37] sub6
  Which of the following is NOT correct about the Airy and Pratt concepts of isostasy?
  Airy assumes uniform crustal density but varying crustal thickness. | *In Pratt's concept, higher blocks are denser. | Pratt assumes varying crustal densities with a uniform depth of compensation. | In Airy's concept, mountains possess deep roots.
  x: In Pratt's concept higher blocks are lighter, not denser. The other three statements correctly describe Airy (uniform density, varying thickness, deep roots) and Pratt (varying density, common compensation depth).
[nc612926f77d] sub6
  Who proposed the geosynclinal theory associated with long, shallow sediment-filled troughs between rigid forelands?
  *Kober | Wegener | Airy | Pratt
  x: Kober proposed the geosynclinal theory. It describes long, shallow sinking sea troughs between rigid forelands or kratogens, where sediments accumulate before being compressed into mountains.
[n256aacfac7a] sub6
  In Kober's geosynclinal theory, which is the first stage, marked by sedimentation?
  Orogenesis | Gliptogenesis | *Lithogenesis | Epeirogenesis
  x: Kober's stages are lithogenesis (sedimentation), orogenesis (folding and mountain building) and gliptogenesis (uplift and denudation).
[fb8bb8dcb06fc] sub6
  In Kober's geosynclinal theory, what are the rigid forelands bordering a geosyncline called?
  Nappes | Horsts | *Kratogens | Grabens
  x: Kober's geosynclines are long shallow troughs between rigid kratogens that are squeezed into mountains.
[fe797d9b4032f] sub6
  What does Pratt's concept of isostasy assume?
  *Varying density with the same compensation depth | Uniform density with varying thickness | Mountains have deep roots | Uniform density and uniform thickness
  x: Pratt assumed varying density and the same compensation depth, so higher blocks are lighter; Airy assumed uniform density and deep roots.

=== NOTES 4:9 | Volcanoes – Types, Materials, Landforms, Global Distribution
## SUB 0 | Famous Volcanoes and Their Countries
[9:0:0] TABLE head: Volcano | Country / place | Note
  [9:0:0.0] Mount St. Helens | USA (Washington) | Active stratovolcano; great eruption 1980 (UPSC 2005, asked twice)
  [9:0:0.1] Mauna Loa | Hawaii, USA | Active shield volcano; world's largest active volcano by volume; erupted Nov 2022, first time in 38 years (since 1984)
  [9:0:0.2] Mauna Kea | Hawaii, USA | Dormant shield; 4,207 m above sea level but over 10,000 m from its base on the sea floor → tallest mountain base-to-peak (APPSC FSO)
  [9:0:0.3] Stromboli | Italy (Lipari/Aeolian Is.) | Active, erupts every few minutes → "Lighthouse of the Mediterranean"
  [9:0:0.4] Etna, Vesuvius | Italy (Etna in Sicily) | Active
  [9:0:0.5] Cotopaxi | Ecuador (Andes) | Active stratovolcano
  [9:0:0.6] Tungurahua | Ecuador (near Baños) | Active; UNESCO Global Geopark, 10 April 2025 (UPSC 2026)
  [9:0:0.7] Whakaari / White Island | New Zealand | Erupted 9 Dec 2019
  [9:0:0.8] Hekla | Iceland | Mid-Atlantic belt
  [9:0:0.9] Kilimanjaro | Tanzania | Dormant stratovolcano, East African Rift
  [9:0:0.10] Krakatoa | Indonesia | Active (Anak Krakatau)
  [9:0:0.11] Fuji | Japan | Composite; classed active (last 1707)
  [9:0:0.12] Volcan de Fuego | Guatemala | Erupted again June 2026; Cocos Plate beneath the Caribbean Plate
  [9:0:0.13] Popocatepetl | Mexico | First 3D image of its magma system (pools down to 18 km)
  [9:0:0.14] Hayli Gubbi | Ethiopia | Shield; erupted Nov 2025 after ~10,000–12,000 years
[9:0:1] - **Active volcanoes (UPPSC 2007):** Cotopaxi, Etna, Fujiyama – **Aconcagua is not** an active volcano.
[9:0:2] - FRO match: **Hekla – Iceland, Kilimanjaro – Tanzania, Mauna Loa – Hawaii, Krakatoa – Indonesia**.
[9:0:3] ~~ Sources: PMF Physical Geography 02 p.46, 54, 58 (pp.133-145); NextGen Rocket World Geography p.2; USGS HVO (Mauna Loa 2022); UNESCO Global Geoparks 2025; app notes 4:9 sub 2, 4
[9:0:4] - **Fujiyama** (3,776 m, Japan's highest peak) is described in books as **dormant** – last eruption **1707** (Japan officially classes it active).
[9:0:5] - **World's highest active volcano: Ojos del Salado** (6,893 m, Andes, Chile–Argentina border).
[9:0:6] - **Mount St. Helens** was called the "**Fujiyama of America**" for its symmetrical cone before the 1980 eruption.
[9:0:7] - **Krakatoa** (Sunda Strait, Indonesia) erupted in **1883**: great tsunamis (~36,000 dead) and a year of global cooling.
[9:0:8] - **Vesuvius** (near Naples) buried **Pompeii and Herculaneum in AD 79**.
[9:0:9] - **Mount Etna** (Sicily): **Europe's largest and most active volcano**, about **3,300 m**; **UNESCO World Heritage Site in 2013**.
[9:0:10] - **Kilauea** (Hawaii) is usually called the **world's most active volcano** (near-continuous eruption 1983–2018, active again since 2020).
## SUB 1 | Volcanoes in India
[9:1:0] TABLE head: Feature | Location | Status
  [9:1:0.0] Barren Island | Andaman Sea | India's only active volcano; active again from 1991 (after ~200 years), later eruptions 2017, 2022, Sep–Oct 2025
  [9:1:0.1] Narcondam | Andaman Sea, ~150 km NE of Barren | Dormant (PMF: probably extinct); no recorded eruption; Narcondam Hornbill
  [9:1:0.2] Baratang | Middle Andaman | Mud volcanoes (and limestone caves); activity 2 Oct 2025
  [9:1:0.3] Diglipur | North Andaman | Mud volcano: mud, water, methane
  [9:1:0.4] Dhinodhar | Kutch, Gujarat | Extinct volcanic hill
  [9:1:0.5] Dhosi Hill, Tosham | Haryana | Extinct volcanic hills
  [9:1:0.6] Deccan Traps | Maharashtra plateau; AP – Prakasam, Palnadu, Guntur, Rajahmundry traps (Godavari) | Fissure flood basalt, end of Cretaceous (~65 million years); Reunion hotspot
[9:1:1] - **India's two volcanic islands:** **Barren and Narcondam** (State PSC).
[9:1:2] - The famous **eruption in Indian territory** is **Barren Island** (Grama Sachivalayam 2019 offered only Narcondam, Semeru – Java, Darjeeling, Kedarnath → option added).
[9:1:3] - **Mud volcanoes** erupt mud, water and gases (methane, CO₂, nitrogen), **not lava**; driven by tectonic pressure on the **Andaman Arc subduction zone**. APPSC 2021: mud volcanoes = **Baratang**; Barren = magmatic.
[9:1:4] - **No volcanoes in the Himalaya or the Peninsula** today.
[9:1:5] ~~ Sources: PMF Physical Geography 02 p.69 (p.156); NextGen Rocket General Geography p.40; NextGen Indian Geography 06 p.3-5 (Andaman map, labels garbled); Smithsonian GVP (Barren, Narcondam); app notes 4:9 sub 7
[9:1:6] - **Barren Island** lies east of the Andaman Islands in the Andaman Sea – part of the **Bay of Bengal** in exam keys.
## SUB 2 | Magma, Lava and Volcanic Materials
[9:2:0] - **Magma** = molten rock **below** the surface (~600–1,200°C, in a magma chamber); **lava** = magma at the surface. "Molten material inside the Earth" = magma → "None of the above" when only lava, basalt, obsidian are offered (UKPSC 2006).
[9:2:1] - Solidified magma/lava → **igneous rocks**.
[9:2:2] TABLE head: Acidic (felsic) lava | Basic (mafic) lava
  [9:2:2.0] Silica-rich, viscous; solidifies faster, does not flow far | Silica-poor, fluid, flows far
  [9:2:2.1] Explosive eruptions; steep cones, stratovolcanoes | Quiet outpourings; shield volcanoes, lava plateaus (basalt)
[9:2:3] - **More silica → faster solidification** (APPSC Dental Asst Surgeons).
[9:2:4] - **Products of eruptions (UPSC 2024 → all four):** pyroclastic debris, ash and dust, **nitrogen** and **sulphur** compounds; gases = **water vapour (most abundant)** > CO₂ > SO₂. Pyroclasts: ash, lapilli, bombs; pyroclastic flows and **lahars** (mudflows) come from stratovolcanoes.
[9:2:5] - SO₂ aerosols scatter sunlight and **cool the Earth for years**; ash falls back within weeks.
[9:2:6] - NDA 2016: eruptions are **accompanied by earthquakes** and **emit water vapour and dust** – both true, but II does **not explain** I.
[9:2:7] # Monitoring instruments (APPSC 2018)
[9:2:8] TABLE head: Instrument | Measures
  [9:2:8.0] Tiltmeter | Ground tilt over faults and volcanoes
  [9:2:8.1] Creepmeter | Fault slip
  [9:2:8.2] Saffir–Simpson scale | Hurricane intensity
  [9:2:8.3] Anemometer | Wind speed (wind vane gives direction) – the wrong pair
[9:2:9] ~~ Sources: NextGen Rocket General Geography p.31-32; PMF Physical Geography 02 p.44, 55, 83; app notes 4:9 sub 0, 1
## SUB 3 | Volcanic Landforms, Hot Springs and Geysers
[9:3:0] - **Volcanic cone = basic (constructional) landform** (UKPSC 2016, asked twice); residual mountain, monadnock, erosional waterfall are erosional forms.
[9:3:1] TABLE head: Intrusive form | Shape / position
  [9:3:1.0] Batholith | Largest, deep dome-like mass; exposed only after erosion
  [9:3:1.1] Laccolith | Dome-shaped body fed by a pipe-like conduit from below
  [9:3:1.2] Lopolith | Saucer-shaped, sagging in the centre
  [9:3:1.3] Phacolith | Wavy mass at the base of synclines or crest of anticlines
  [9:3:1.4] Sill / sheet | Near-horizontal along rock layers (thin = sheet)
  [9:3:1.5] Dyke | Near-vertical wall across layers; feeders of the Deccan Traps
[9:3:2] - **Basaltic lava plateaus** (Deccan) weather into fertile **black soil**.
[9:3:3] - **Geysers** = hot springs that erupt periodically. **Yellowstone National Park, USA** (Wyoming; 1872) has the most geysers (500+), including **Old Faithful** (asked twice).
[9:3:4] TABLE head: Hot spring | State
  [9:3:4.0] Manikaran | Himachal Pradesh (Kullu valley)
  [9:3:4.1] Tattapani | Chhattisgarh
  [9:3:4.2] Taptapani | Odisha
  [9:3:4.3] Vajreshwari | Maharashtra
  [9:3:4.4] Puga Valley, Chumathang | Ladakh (not Himachal)
[9:3:5] - **Puga:** ONGC drilled India's **first and deepest geothermal wells** (to 1,000 m) for a 1 MW pilot. GSI lists **340** geothermal sites.
[9:3:6] ~~ Sources: PMF Physical Geography 02 p.70 (geysers); app notes 4:9 sub 5, 6
[9:3:7] - **Sill** = near-horizontal sheet of intrusive igneous rock along the bedding; a dome-shaped mound is a **laccolith**, not a phacolith.
## SUB 4 | Types of Volcanoes – Activity and Form
[9:4:0] TABLE head: By activity | Meaning | Example
  [9:4:0.0] Active | Erupts fairly frequently | Barren Island, Stromboli, Etna, Mauna Loa
  [9:4:0.1] Dormant | Quiet for long, capable of erupting again | Narcondam, Kilimanjaro, Mauna Kea
  [9:4:0.2] Extinct | No eruption expected | Dhinodhar, Mount Kenya
[9:4:1] # By form
[9:4:2] TABLE head: Form | Shape | Examples
  [9:4:2.0] Shield | Broad, gentle slopes of fluid basalt | Mauna Loa, Hayli Gubbi
  [9:4:2.1] Composite / strato | Steep cone of alternating lava and ash; explosive | Fuji, Vesuvius, St. Helens, Fuego, Taftan
  [9:4:2.2] Cinder cone | Small steep cone of ash, cinders, scoria around one vent | Cerro Negro (Nicaragua)
  [9:4:2.3] Complex | Several vents, overlapping cones | Taal, Dukono
  [9:4:2.4] Caldera | Cauldron-shaped hollow after the chamber empties and the summit collapses | –
[9:4:3] - Crater = funnel-shaped mouth at the top of a cone; caldera = much larger collapse hollow.
[9:4:4] ~~ Sources: PMF Physical Geography 02 p.54-55 (pp.141-142); app notes 4:9 sub 0, 2
[9:4:5] - **Mount Aso** (Kyushu, **Japan**) has one of the **world's largest calderas** (~25 × 18 km).
[9:4:6] - **Philippine volcanoes:** **Taal** (complex volcano – an island in a caldera lake), **Mayon** (near-perfect symmetrical composite cone), **Pinatubo** (1991 eruption).
[9:4:7] - **Composite (strato) volcanoes** are the commonest type – about **60%** of the Earth's volcanoes.
[9:4:8] - **Eruption styles (mild → violent):** Hawaiian (lava fountains) → **Strombolian** (frequent, mild bursts) → **Vulcanian** (more explosive, ash clouds) → Pelean → **Plinian** (Vesuvius AD 79).
## SUB 5 | Global Distribution of Volcanoes
[9:5:0] TABLE head: Belt | Volcanoes
  [9:5:0.0] Circum-Pacific "Ring of Fire" | Most eruptions and earthquakes – Fuji, St. Helens, Cotopaxi, Tungurahua, Fuego, Mayon, Pinatubo, Krakatau, Whakaari, Popocatepetl
  [9:5:0.1] Mid-Atlantic belt | Iceland – Hekla
  [9:5:0.2] Mediterranean belt | Vesuvius, Etna, Stromboli
  [9:5:0.3] Intraplate hotspots | Hawaii (Mauna Loa)
  [9:5:0.4] East African Rift | Kilimanjaro, Afar triple junction
[9:5:1] - **No extrusive volcanoes in the Himalayas** (CDS 2018): continent–continent collision yields no magma; Alaska, Rockies, Andes lie on subduction margins.
[9:5:2] - Stromboli = Mediterranean, **not** East African Rift.
[9:5:3] ~~ Sources: PMF Physical Geography 02 p.69 (p.156); app notes 4:9 sub 3
[9:5:4] - **Indonesia:** over **17,000 islands** (17,508) and about **130 active volcanoes** – the most of any country; "over 150 active volcanoes" is wrong.

  x: Water vapour is the most abundant volcanic gas. The mantle is the layer below the crust, a volcano releases lava, ash and gases, and magma produces volcanism.

  x: Puga Valley and Chumathang are in Ladakh, not Himachal Pradesh; the wells reached 1,000 m for a 1 MW pilot.
[ga7081381d11f] sub3
  In which State is the Manikaran hot spring located?
  *Himachal Pradesh | Uttarakhand | Odisha | Maharashtra
  x: Manikaran hot spring is in the Kullu valley of Himachal Pradesh.
[gd6af7b07a165] sub3
  About how many geothermal sites has the Geological Survey of India listed?
  140 | 240 | *340 | 440
  x: The GSI lists about 340 geothermal sites in India.
[g90d27bd214ff] sub3
  In which country is Old Faithful, the best-known geyser of Yellowstone National Park, located?
  Iceland | New Zealand | *USA | Canada
  x: Old Faithful is in Yellowstone National Park, USA, which has more geysers than anywhere else on Earth.
[ge3a44597f635] sub3
  Which place has the largest number of geysers in the world?
  *Yellowstone National Park, USA | Iceland's Haukadalur valley | Rotorua, New Zealand | Kamchatka, Russia
  x: Yellowstone National Park has more geysers than anywhere else on Earth, over 500.
[n12fba8c25b5] sub3
  Which one of the following is an extrusive volcanic landform?
  Lopolith | *Lava plateau | Phacolith | Batholith
  x: Lava plateaus, cinder and composite cones, craters, calderas and lava flows are extrusive volcanic landforms. Batholiths, lopoliths and phacoliths are intrusive forms.
[n32877bd0d26] sub3
  What is a vertical wall of igneous rock cutting across the rock beds called?
  Sill | Laccolith | Lopolith | *Dyke
  x: A dyke is a vertical wall cutting across beds; a sill is a horizontal sheet parallel to the beds. Dykes fed the Deccan Traps.
[n0e3becf457d] sub3
  What is a large, often dome-shaped mass of granite occurring at great depth and exposed after denudation called?
  Sill | Phacolith | *Batholith | Lopolith
  x: A batholith is a huge mass of granite emplaced at depth. It may become exposed after denudation removes the overlying rocks.
[nd3c3e51c80d] sub3
  Which one of the following pairs is correctly matched?
  Batholith — saucer-shaped intrusive body | Lopolith — wavy mass in a folded structure | *Phacolith — wavy mass at the base of a syncline or top of an anticline | Laccolith — vertical wall cutting across rock beds
  x: A phacolith is a wavy intrusive mass occurring at the base of a syncline or the top of an anticline. A lopolith is saucer-shaped, a laccolith is dome-shaped with a flat base, and a dyke is a vertical wall.
[ff49988077150] sub3
  What is a dome-shaped intrusive body with a flat base, fed by a pipe from below, called?
  *Laccolith | Phacolith | Lopolith | Batholith
  x: Domes with a flat base are laccoliths; a phacolith is a wavy mass, so calling phacoliths large domed mounds is wrong.
[n519b61e6b9a] sub4
  What is a cauldron-shaped depression formed when a large eruption empties the magma chamber and the summit collapses called?
  *Caldera | Crater | Sill | Dyke
  x: A caldera forms by summit collapse after the magma chamber empties; a crater is the funnel-shaped mouth at the top of a cone.
[fc0764fb9937b] sub4
  What is the funnel-shaped mouth at the top of a volcanic cone called?
  Caldera | *Crater | Sill | Dyke
  x: A crater is the funnel-shaped mouth of a cone; a caldera is the larger hollow left when the summit collapses.
[nca02b86cf4f] sub4
  What are steep volcanic cones built from alternating layers of lava and ash, with explosive eruptions, called?
  *Composite (strato) volcanoes | Shield volcanoes | Fissure volcanoes | Mud volcanoes
  x: Composite or stratovolcanoes are steep, consist of alternating lava and ash layers and are associated with explosive eruptions.
[nfd143508583] sub4
  What is a volcano that has been quiet for a long time but may erupt again called?
  Active | *Dormant | Extinct | Composite
  x: A dormant volcano has been quiet for long but may erupt again; an extinct volcano is one from which no eruption is expected.
[fa804e1c1a3a1] sub4
  What is a small, steep cone of loose ash, cinders and scoria built around a single vent called?
  Shield volcano | Composite volcano | *Cinder cone | Complex volcano
  x: A cinder cone is a small steep cone of loose ash, cinders and scoria around one vent, e.g. Cerro Negro in Nicaragua.
[fd44a49c7384c] sub4
  Taal and Dukono, with several vents and overlapping cones, are examples of which type of volcano?
  Shield volcano | Cinder cone | Composite volcano | *Complex volcano
  x: Complex volcanoes have several vents and overlapping cones, as at Taal and Dukono.
[gd1f3af34be03] sub4
  Which type of volcano is Mauna Loa in Hawaii?
  *Shield volcano | Composite volcano | Cinder cone | Complex volcano
  x: Mauna Loa is a shield volcano with broad, gentle slopes built of fluid basalt.
[g4d040a06ceb6] sub4
  Which of the following is a composite (strato) volcano?
  Mauna Loa | Hayli Gubbi | *Fuji | Cerro Negro
  x: Fuji is a composite volcano of alternating lava and ash layers; Mauna Loa and Hayli Gubbi are shield volcanoes, Cerro Negro a cinder cone.
[n5657503d44f] sub4
  Composite or stratovolcanoes constitute approximately what share of the Earth's volcanoes?
  25% | 40% | *60% | 80%
  x: Composite volcanoes, with viscous lava, steep cones and explosive eruptions, make up approximately 60% of Earth's volcanoes.
[n7f6c6b23220] sub4
  Which of the following pairs is correctly matched?
  Mauna Loa — caldera formed by explosive collapse | *Mount Aso — one of the world's largest calderas | Etna — complex volcano with several overlapping vents only | Dukono — shield volcano with gentle basaltic slopes
  x: Mount Aso is one of the world's largest calderas. Mauna Loa is a shield volcano, while Dukono is listed as a complex volcano.
[n440bee9aba6] sub4
  Which of the following is correctly matched with a complex or compound volcano?
  Taal — Japan | Dukono — Guatemala | *Taal — Philippines | Mount Aso — Philippines
  x: Taal in the Philippines is a complex or compound volcano. Dukono is in Indonesia, while Mount Aso is a caldera in Japan.
[na89e677d617] sub4
  Which statement correctly distinguishes Strombolian and Vulcanian eruptions?
  Vulcanian eruptions consist only of gentle lava flows. | *Strombolian eruptions are frequent and mild, whereas Vulcanian eruptions are more explosive. | Strombolian eruptions are always more explosive than Vulcanian eruptions. | Both terms refer exclusively to dormant volcanoes.
  x: Strombolian eruptions involve frequent, mild bursts of bombs and ash, whereas Vulcanian eruptions are more explosive.

  x: Mount Aso in Japan is a caldera, among the world's largest.

  Indonesia | Japan | *Philippines | Papua New Guinea
  x: Mayon in the Philippines is a symmetrical composite cone on the Pacific Ring of Fire.
[n24183562e77] sub5
  Which belt is associated with the greatest concentration of volcanic eruptions and earthquakes?
  Mediterranean belt | Mid-Atlantic belt | *Pacific Ring of Fire | East African Rift alone
  x: The Pacific Ring of Fire has the greatest concentration of volcanic eruptions and earthquakes. It includes volcanoes such as Fuji and Mount St. Helens.
[nb6521674df5] sub5
  Which of the following pairs is NOT correctly matched?
  Intraplate hotspot — Hawaii | Mid-Atlantic belt — Hekla | Mediterranean belt — Vesuvius and Etna | *East African Rift — Stromboli
  x: Stromboli belongs to the Mediterranean volcanic belt. The East African Rift is associated with Kilimanjaro and the Afar triple junction.
[fbc82156190c2] sub5
  Hekla volcano of Iceland lies on which belt?
  Mediterranean belt | *Mid-Atlantic belt | Pacific Ring of Fire | East African Rift
  x: Hekla in Iceland is on the Mid-Atlantic belt.

  With which of the following is Mauna Loa in Hawaii associated?
  The Mid-Atlantic belt | The Mediterranean belt | *An intraplate hotspot | The East African Rift
  x: Hawaii's Mauna Loa is an intraplate hotspot volcano.
[f2e0045f1571c] sub5
  Which of the following is NOT correct about volcanic belts?
  Kilimanjaro lies in the East African Rift | Fuji lies on the Pacific Ring of Fire | Hekla lies on the Mid-Atlantic belt | *Stromboli lies in the East African Rift
  x: Stromboli belongs to the Mediterranean belt, not the East African Rift.
